PLEASANT PARAGRAPHS.

-- ALL is fair in love and War ; in other words, all Is fair before and after marriage. ORGANISED charity is a sort of condensed milk V human kindness. THAT 9 ALL.—Beggar: "Sind gentleman, I beg your pardon--° Gent (promptly) : "Granted. I thought you were begging for money." A BOY swallowed a revolver cartridge one day last week, and his mother doesn't dare to whip ftim for fear he'll go off. WHEN. a man says," Of course it's no business of inine," it is a sure sign that he is going to do the best he can to make it his business. CracmisTaNcEs are such that many a man cares for relatives for whom he really cares nothing. BINKS (reading) : " Women can endure pain better than men.' Mr. Binks : Who says that—a doctor or a shoemaker ?" "WHEN she jilted you did you declare to her that you could never love another?' "Oh, yes. I didn't forget my manners entirely." THE pessimist is a person whose mind is divided between the belief that things are as bad as they can be, and the expectation that they will be worse. TIIERE is nothing poetical about money. Dis- tant prospects of it lend no enchantment to the view. THERE is a great deal to be had in this world by a:al.:Mg yourself disagreeable, if you do it judici- ously. A PROPHET never forgets his predictions if they come true; and if they don't his friends won t let him forget them. IN the first transports of delight, the 'happy father rushed into the room, exclaiming: "I've got a son! It's a boy !" HE is a mighty meek man that can patiently hold the baby while his wife puts in a couple of hours at the piano learning the latest lullaby. CLIENT: "What makes you so certain that you will be able to upset the will?" Lawyer (in a whisper) : "I drew it." THE man who should invent a machine so that people could put a penny in the slot and pick out a name for the baby would surely make a fortune —it would take so many to get a name to suit. "I CAN'T pay this bill, doctor. It's exorbitant. I'm no better than I was, either." "That's because you didn't take my advice." " Ah—well —of course, if I didn't take it I don't owe you for it. Thanks. Good morning."

Do you know him?" asked a gentleman of an Irish friend the other day, in speaking of a third person. "Know him!" said the Irishman, "I knew him when his father was a little boy!" "UNCLE: "What have you learned at school to-day, Bobby: "Just how to take the back off my history and fix a real good Indian story into it, so the teacher can't find out that I ain't studying." Lotman up suddenly, she beheld the bearded face of a man with a gleaming knife between his teeth. Then she fainted. It was no wonder, for she had been carefully reared, and had never seen any one eat in that manner before. HcreELEss.—" I have lost my ascendancy over my husband completely." " Has he ceased to love you?" "Oh, no; but I can't scare him with my hysterics any more." FIRST MAN: "Why did you ask Blibbs just now to lend you five bob? You had plenty of money this morning." Second Man: "I have plenty now; but I knew Blibbs was going to ask me for some." You never know how dear things are until you buy them, nor how cheap they are until you sell them. IN the Wild West a man killed his wife an axe. The reporter's headlines announced "Being married first and axed afterwards." A BOY in one of our public schools having been told that a reptile "is an animal that creeps," being asked the name of one, promptly replied, "A baby.' SOMEONE asked an old lady about a sermon. "Could you remember it?" "Remember it? La, no; the minister couldn't remember it himself. He had to have it written down!" THE man who never knows when he ii beaten would be perfectly happy if he could get rid of the suspicion that other people msy he better informed. JOTTINGS.

SHE (on her voyage) : "What is that place down there?" He: "Why, that is the steerage." She: "And does it take all those people to make the boat go straight?" PEYSER : "I hear it's fashionable now to wear overcoats longer than formerly." Cakesy : "If that's the case, I'll be able to get another winter out of mine." IF a woman would look first in the place where she knows she didn't put a thing, instead of the place where she thinks she did, she would save lots of time. PROVED.—She: "I don't believe you think half so much of me as Tom Dinsley does. He tells me he could die for me." Ho: That's nothing. I love you well enough to live with you." HIS DESIRE.—HoteI Clerk: "We can give you all the home comforts here." Uncle Abner (from Squeehawke): "Mister, I want more'n that when I come to a hotel. I kin git home comforts at hum." JOHNNY'S SUGGF,STION.—Mother : "I just have to drive Ethel to the piano, and even then she won't half practice." Second Doctor: "The patient has lately taking to writing jokes." SHE trembled with rage. "Insult—insult !" he moaned; "and yet I cannot raise a hand, unless "—a ray of hope appeared—"someone will come along and unhook my waist in the back." Suitor: "Would you con- eider a man in debt who borrows money from his father-in-law?" Aunt Dinah: "I shad say dat was a miracle." Uncle Pete (producing a pair of chickens): " Dat's right, a'nty ; doe's just what it wuz, a miracle. Nothin' short ob a miracle wud have made de colonel fo'git teh lock de hennery do'." MANAGER (examining candidate for district mes- senger) : "Can you read ? " Boy "In course I kin read." Manager. "Oh, I've got that down fine," said the would-be soubrette. "I typewrote for three years for a man who was always telling me funny stories about his little boy." A RECENT burglar-scare led to a rather farcical incident in a Wolverhampton suburb. Armed with thick sticks both crept cautiously into the passage, and both rained blows on the man they each found there. The one nearly succeeded in murdering the other. THE

LADIES' LETTER.

MY DEAREST NELLIE,

I may tell you that a great deal of brown will be worn this autumn and winter. This colour having been a little neglected of late will be cordially welcomed, for it is very becoming. Its chief fault is its tendency to fade. After a few wearings a brown gown of woollen material (even the best) is apt to show a decided contrast between the surface exposed and the inside of the pleats or folds. However, fashion which exists for the rich, does not pay any heed to these little matters. We shall have brown trimmed with jet passementcrie, and brown trimmed with jet and gold. 1 vn always brings in plenty of yellow in its train the waistcoats of our dressy brown gowns m of daffodil yellow satin or cloth, quite plain, or elaborately trimmed with stripes or straps of jet passementerie, which is to be very handsome and much worn. Daffodil satin will be a favourite evening material for tea-gowns and low-necked dresses. White faille, trimmed with daffodil satin, will be no uncommon mixture for elaborate winter dinner gowns.

However, , daven't got over summer yet, and, I of course, you will want me to tell you about holi- day dresses. The smartest yachting-gowns, then, are in coarse diagonal alpaca, grey in colour, the seam and edges piped with white suede, the coat revers being outlined with a strap of suede. A grey sailor hat, or better still, a grey or white cloth peaked cap, will be worn with these yacht- ing dresses, which have quite superseded serge. Comparatively few of us, however, can enjoy yachting, either in the blue waters of the Solent or on the silver surface of Father Thames or any other river. The great majority of pleasure-lovers have tennis and the recently-revived croquet open to them, for over and above club grounds many villas and country residences have lawns large enough for tennis, and sometimes for croquet as well. Girls who do not pursue tennis as the one object of life in all weathers are rather modifying their costumes this year. Flannel and serge are not quite de rigueur, and they are wearing plain white or blue serge skirts with blouses. Here is a white cambric tennis shirt, with bands of blue cambric and embroidery insertion work in blue cotton, let in. Wise virgins will, however, wear woollen stockingette bodices under their cool cam- bric shirts and blouses. I have seen a very cool- looking grey flannel dress, with crossed tennis rackets embroidered in crimson silk all round the wide skirt, and another couple on the breast of the bodice, crimson silk sash and neckband. But wide skirts are not generally beloved by players.

Here is a pretty but unpretentious dress of grey and white worn at one of last week's fashionable weddings. The main portion was in a rich mat grey silk, in good contrast with the glaces so much worn. Skirt, sash, sleeves, and neckband were all grey, but the corsage itself was of white striped figured brocade. Quantities of such brocades are to be bought in short lengths at the sales, and they could hardly be put to a better purpose. A pretty shady black chip hat was worn with it, with a row of sweet little pink roses under the brim and loops of wide pink straw plait outside, in addition to Four black plumes. The crown of this hat is dis- tinctly Tyrolese. Millinery is very pretty just now, and for ordi- nary wear it is pleasingly simple. One of the newest and nicest shapes has a crown something Like a Tam o' Shanter, and the narrow brim is turned up round the edge, the trimming is merely a twist of ribbon, and the loops at the side, mingled with some blades of wide grass, are all caught with a tiny buckle. Then there is a toque of black rice straw, with loops of striped ribbon in front, and a cluster of verbena and broad-leaved grass at the back. A picture hat which looks smart is in black chip edged with fancy straw, trimmed with wide black satin bows at each side, shaded heliotrope roses and black ospreys.

For country-hot. dresses Ite remains fashionable as ever, for the linings of coloured costumes, for collars, revers, vests, blouses, and even for gloves. The latest novelty is a belt in white kid or suede, that is pretty on most bodices provided the waist is either slender or well rounded. Passementcrie will be much used as a trimming for the skirts and bodices of demisaison costumes in mohair and alpaca. Braiding and raised embroideries, on bands of white or coloured cloth, are also being prepared, and are prophetic of the downfall of the blouse, such trimmings being only possible on a well-fitting bodice, and of a possible alteration in the shape of skirts; but this change lies in the future, and for the present all skirts are cut in godet shape, and all are stiffly lined.

Your loving JENNig.

SATURDAY, SEPTEMBER 7. 1895.

There are seven millionaire-editors in New York. Epping Forest is the largest pu`slic recreation ground in the world. The heart of a Greenland whale is a yard in dia- meter. Little oak trees, an inch and a half high, are grown by Chinese gardeners. They take root in thimbles. The word" flirtation," according te Lord Chesterfield, was manufactured by Lady Frances Shirley, who used it in the exact sense attached to it to-day. The value of the imports of potatoes in England year y is estimated at about six hundred thousand pounds. Do not disfigure the hands with caustic to remove warts, but touch them with strong soda water several Mmes a day. They will disappear. The very poor of Berlin are better housed than those of any other large city in the world. The German capi- tal is really without "slums." In 1182 the sands were heated by the sun to such an extent that ins few minutes eggs could be cooked in them. It is not generally known that it is illegal to blow wind instruments in the streets. Unfortunately, this law is but feebly administered. A Chinese custom is the throwing into the ocean of thousands of pieces of paper when friends are about to sail. Each piece bears writt monit a prayer. At a recent fashionable weddin ! in Chicago, the bride, a widow, was given away at the altar by her son, whose age is ten years. We wear away two inches of shoe-leather in a year. A pair of boots that would "last a lifetime" would,conse- quently, have to be provided with soles from eight to nine feet thick. The ultra swells of London are trying to introduce the fashion of wearing black satin bows with drooping ends to connect the cuffs, instead of the well-known sleeve- links. The ribbon must be one inch wide. The sting of a bee, when compared with the point of a fine needle, under a powerful microscore, is hardly dis- cernible. The point of the needle appears to be about an inch in diameter. A new outer sole is being tried on the boots of the Ger- man soldiers. It consists of a paste of linseed oil, var- nish, and iron filings, which is said to render the sole flexible and to make it more durable than the new alum- inium bootnails. Among the Turks bath-money forms an item in every marriage contract, the husband engaging to allow his wife a certain sum for bathing purposes. If it be with- held, she has only to go before the cadi and turn her slipper upside down. If the complaint be not then re- dressed it is ground for divorce. Women doctors should certainly rank as reliable auth- orities upon such a subject as the effect of bicycling upon the health of their own sex, and it is therefore very interesting to know that the result of a gleaning of the opinions of eighteen lady doctors of the United States is an overwhelming verdict in favour of the fashionable amusement.

E KAMINE-

A Few Dent's.

Don't wear a veil with; hole in it. It gives a look of poverty that there is no excuse for. Veils cost little, and at the worst you can go without one. Don't wear a hat too young unless you wish to look old. A sailor hat can be confidently recommended as calculated to make any mature woman look like her grandmother. Don't cut yourself in two near the knees with a coat that strikes you about there if you are a short woman. Nothinc,, detracts more from an appearance of 12, igh-. Don't forget that pointed openings of the dress at the throat at becoming only to slender women.

Drying an Umbrella. During the frequent use of umbrellas in the showers we should keep in mind the oft-repeated caution con- cerning care in drying them. They will last much longer if they are always placed, when wet, with the handle downward to dry. Toe moisture then falls from the end of the frame and the fabric dries uniformly. When it stands handle upwards, which is commonly the onset the top of the umbrella holds the rnoi ture and not only takes a long time to dry, but it soon injures the silk or other fabrics used for the cover and ru:ts the steels. This is the cause of this part of the umbrella wearing out before any other part. Umbrella cases are also responsible for the rapid wear of the silk. The constant friction eauQes the tiny holes that appear so provokingly early. When not in use leave the umbrella loose.

===== 0002601_18950907_art0016.txt | included=true | included =====
EVERYBODY'S ENEMY.

A famous physician and author entitles one of his books" The Demon of Dyspepsia." It is a bright idea. Demons (or devils) are supposed to be enemies of the human race. Dyspepsia answers the definition com- pletely. Body, mind, and spirit perish through the power of it. War, famine, and pestilence—bad things, all of them. Yet only see 1 War ends, famine ends,pestilence ends. But when does dyspepsia end? When is a com- munity, or any family, free from it ? Never. At all seasons, in all climates, it is on hand, and always busy. And what a mimic this demon is ! One after another he can assume every character in the play. He is a whole tragedy company. We used to speak of rheumatism, gout, bronchitis, liver, kidney, and lung ailments, nervous affections, Sze., as separate diseases, local in origin and treatment. We know better now. These are but names of some of the disguises worn by the Demon of Dyepepsia—aome of the parts he plays. Take this from a letter : " Ever since I was four- teen years of age (eleven years ago) I had suffered ['rem hot flushes, headache, aad dizziness. In January, 1885, I was in service at the late Squire Heatley's Hazelmere Lodge, near High Wyeembe. My appetite fell away. and everything I ate gave me pain at the chest and under the shoulder-blades. I had a bad taste in the mouth, and thick phlegm covered my tongue and teeth. My akin was sallow, eyes of a yellow hue, and I had great oppression and pain at my tight side and across the stomach. I was also troubled with wind, and at times violent palpitation, so that I Feared that I had heart disease. My breath became short, and I had to stop and rest when doing my house work. Next a nasty cough fastened on me, and I shook as I laboured to clear the phlegm away. Bad night-sweats followed, and my linen was wet with them every morning. A doctor who was attending Squire Heatley was called to see me. He attended me over two months, yet I got worse. He said my liver was dis- eased, and if ever I got better, it would be a long time .first. I took more than a drzen bottles of his medicine without benefit, and at the end of March, 1886, I left my situation and returned to my home at Widma-re End near fligh Wycombe. Bucks, "My mother was shocked at my condition. I could scarcely crawl about. I could only get upstairs one step at a time, rosting on each step ; and every breath I drew hort me. During the day I had to lie down on the couch. At night F went to bed early, and could not rise before noon. My mother and brother thought I had consumption and would never get well. "One day my mother said : ' Ada, why don't you try Mother espieeFe Curative Syrup? You know what good it did your brother Thomas.' I sent to the stores in White Hart-street, Wycombe, and got bottle. After having need it for only a few days I felt better, and gained a little strength. After this, keeping on with the Syrup, I had no relapse. By the time I had taken six bettlee, the night-eV/eats, the cough, *and all the other bad feelings left me, and I have since enjoyed better health than for eleven years before. I have reason to thick that Mother Seig,el's Syrup saved my life, and wish others to hear of what did so much for MP. I will answer any inquiry. Yours truly (Mrs.) Ada Castle, 5, Farnham-road Cottages, Farnham Common,near Slough, September 3rd, 1892." In this case the Demon mimicked consumption. asthma, and heart disease—which in an organic form at least, Mrs. Castle was not afflicted with. We sae " mimicked " them. Yet the actual truth is these "diseases," with fifty others, are only the natural is- suits and symptoms of Dyspepsia. They are poison berries On the tree of Dyspepsia. Pull up that tree by the roots and they all die with it. And may we not be sure that the success of Mother 'Seigel's great remedy in striking down the demon who threatens us all will he heralded everywhere by the grateful voices of the sufferers whom lshe saves? Indeed we may. iy

OR, THE MARK OF THE BLACK CROSS. -- Br SYLVANIIB COBB, JB.

CHAPTER XL A GREAT AGONY. With Col. Tabor. the governor- general's chief- 4f-staff, Lord Birchmeat had much business • and Ise found it convenient to wait upon that Officer at his private residence. Here he met Mary Wallace, and when he knew her as the niece and ward of his friend, Sir Jasper, he took her at once vto his heart. With a gallantry which was as deli- cate as it was generous and humane, he offered his attendance upon the Promenade, and to places of entertainment, thereby relieving her from the attentions of younger men, who would have been too glad to have shared her companionship. Lady Birchmear, when she knew that the beautiful girl was Sir Jasper's sole heir, destined, if she lived, to inherit one of the fairest estates in England, smiled benignantly upon her, and was profuse in her tokens of love and good-will. Mary Wallace was not quick atreadingtreachery, when it came under the plausible guise of friend. ship. Herself pure and true, she was willing to believe others pure and true, who seemed so. And then, how could she suspect that Lady Birchmear, a woman past the prime of life, and firmly fixed in worldly prosperity, could be else than she professed? How, and in what, could her ladyship wish to deceive her? Mary did not feel the subject worth a serious question; and she came to repose all confidence in Helen Berwick. Her ladyship had heard the story of Arthur Rainsford's passage in the Golden Fleece, and she was not long in discovering that he was Mary's accepted lover. If she now and then hinted at the secret, she did it in a manner so delicate that the maiden was pleased rather than offended; and, finally, by adroit management, she led our heroine to a full and free confession. And thus was Harold Berwick brought into frequent companionship with Mary Wallace. When he first saw her, he was forced to the convic- tion that he had never before beheld anything so beautiful; and when he had been permitted to sit by her side, and to hand her to her place for a drive, he fell desperately in love with her. And with him this love was in reality a species of desperation. It so far absorbed the brutish passions of his nature as to hold them in check, as the mountain torrent is held by an obstructing snag, only gathering more potent force for evil when the bond is broken. He had been dazzled by her beauty before he knew the story of Arthur's love: and when he knew that he who had been his rival at Birchmear was more than his rival here, his heart was filled with passions most venomous and deadly. But long years of evil life, with many a cup broken at his lips, had brought to him a certain degree of caution, and he hid from those around him—from all save his mother—that he hated his step-brother with a mortal hatred. One night, after Sir John had retired, Lady Birchmear and her son met by appointment. There was need of new work, and they had met for con- sultation. They sat down, each seen-dug to understand the other perfectly. After a pause Harold opened the business by asking: "Have you managed to gain anything from Sir John ?" "Gain anything? In what direction ?" Her ladyship had been meditating, and did not readily comprehend the di ift of her son's question. "in the direction of the only thing we ever had to fear," answered Harold, abruptly, and not in the best of humour. "You allude to Arthur Rainsford, my son; but let me assure you in the outset that you have some- thing to fear from your own record. As a vely first requisite to success you must so present you. - self before your step-father that not even a suspicion of wrong-doing can attach to you. You must .07 be officious, but you must he kind and considerate; and, above all, you must put on a garb of modesty and—" "Hold !" cried Harold, with an impatient wave of the hand. "I understand all that." "And," pursued his mother, earnestly, "you must pay heed to the lesson. Do you realise the magnitude of the stake for which you play?" "By heavens! I think I do. I see upon the board the broad acres of Birchmear, its prompt pay- ing tenants, its grand old mansion its mint of gold iu bank, its noble peerage, and its power and grandeur of social position !" "And, on the other hand," whispered her lady- ship, " you may see only Harold Travers—the son of a spendthrift—without position, and without money—cast upon the world—" " Hush ! No more of that, my mother." "Mark me, Harold ! there is no middle ground. You will be either Baron Birchmear or you will be a penniless outcast !" There was a cold, steel-like glitter—a glitter such as the keen blade of a stilletto shoots forth under the glare of the moonbeams—in the eyes of the woman as she thus spoke, and her fingers were clutched upon the arm of her son with masculine strength and energy. As she now sat, with all her thoughts given to the purpose of evil, prepared to adopt any means that might offer towards the accomplishment of her purpose, and also prepared to crush every human barrier that offer obstruction in her way, she looked the very incarnation of diabolism. Her beauty was the beauty of the coiled serpent, and the strength evinced in the clasp of her fingers was tigerish. "And," said Harold, with a fierce contraction of his dark and lowering brow, "Arthur Rains- ford still stands in my way ?" "That," replied her ladyship, with a change in her look and tone, "brings Us direct to our busi- ness; and I can now answer your leading question. I have sounded Sir John deeply and carefully, and I am able to inform you that the burden of his old love for Arthur is returning upon him. Not only has the story of the battle with the pirates worked wonderfully upon him in that direction, but I can see that the soft speech of Mary Wallace is having much influence. In short, Harold, this Gordian knot must be cut !" Harold Berwick swore a terrible oath, and the veins stood out grimly upon his dusky temples. " !if I could but cut the knot that binds HER, to his support! Cannot you help me in that, my mother? If I could win Mary Wallace I" "Harold—lock first to your rival! He will be in Calcutta ere long. Sir Jasper's return is daily expected. If I can, meanwhile, help you with the girl, I will do so; but you must not call upon me in this other direction. Surely, here in this place, with such instrumentalities as lie about on every hand, you should be able to crush the interloper. It were well that he and Sir John never met again !" "I understand," said Harold, without looking up. He seemed to be considering where next his feet should tread ; and be held his hand before him and looked upon it as though he wondered whether blood would soil it. "You will understand," added his mother, with another of those stiletto-like gleams, " that a peerage and a fortune hang in the balance 1 Harold stepped forward and took her by the band—or, rather grasped her with a vice-like grip. "Look to the girl, and I will look to the other He shall never moot Sir John if I can help it 1" "If you can help it!" cried the lady contemp- tuously. "Do you admit ifr at such a time and in such a work? Have you forgotten all the mysteries of Black Town? If your own hand shrinks from the work, tire there not dark hands enough that can be made ready and willing by the touch of gold? Say not, If! Say I WILL I" "Then, may mother," answered Harold, with an oath, "let it be—l WILL! Do we understand each other ?" "I think we do." And they separated. On the following day, at dinner' Lord Birchmear announced that his old friend, Sir Jasper, would shortly arrive in Calcutta. "And I trust," said her ladyship, with a smile of anticipation, "that Arthur will come with him." " Yes," responded Sir John. • And that was all he said. Lady Birchmear dared not push the subject, and it was dropped. Yet she gathered from his very reticence that his heart was with its first love. He would have spoken his enmity freely. It was the deeper feeling that he kept hidden. That evening Lady Birchmear spent an hour with Mary Wallace. She put forth her whole power of soft and winning speech, and the heart of the unsus- pecting maiden was opened so freely that she told over the whole story of her life. While Mary spo'ke her ladyship listened attentively. At length came a word that startled her. Did Lady Birchmear hear aright? She certainly did. But she would understand it more thoroughly. And Mary explained it in full. Her ladyship could not repress the startling effect of the intelligence she had gathered. She sat like one upon whom a blinding glare of light had fallen—as though a lightning flash had streamed

_I

L

before her without—ifs -mite Of-thunder; "Dear lady !" cried Mary, in alarm, "what is It?" "Hush, dear child I shall overcome it ta a moment? It is nothing." "Was it anything I told you ?" the anxious girl asked, seeing that her ladyship still trembled. "My blessed child !" cried Lady Birchmear, taking the gentle maiden to her bosom," I cannot tell you a falsehood. I was moved by the intelli- gence you communicated: but you must not ask me why." "Must not ask you! And you so moved! But perhaps it does not concern me ?" "Alas, my poor girl, it concerns you more than I can tell ! But what am I saying? Let us forget the whole thing, darling, and talk of something else. Your good guardian will soon be here and your heart will then be lightened." Mary Wallace was human, and she could be persistent when her will was aroused. She would not suffer Lady Birchmear to evade the momentous subject in that manner. If there was anything of Importance—anything touching her own welfare— hidden from her, she wished to know it. And she appealed to her ladyship upon the grounds of com- mon humanity, setting aside all considerations of friendship, to explain the mystery. She held her by the hand and implored her. Lady Birchmear turned away and rested her head upon her hand. At length she said, and she simulated great anguish of mind very well: "Mary, you must give me time to think. I dare not speak now. I am sorry I exposed my- self ; but it cannot now be helped. Wait until to- morrow." _

Will you tell me then ?" "If I do tell you then, my child, you will not wonder that I hesitated at first. But be not over- anxious. After all it may turn out to be nothing at all. I may have judged entirely by my own feelings and judged wrongfully." It had been very easy for her ladyship to bid the anxious girl to put away her fears, but the work was not so easy of accomplishment. When Mary had been left dace a great dread had come upon her, and as is always the case in seasons of peril, the terror was just in proportion as the cause was hidden and mystical. At a later hour Lady Birchmear met her son and called him into her dressing-room. "Have you heard further from Sir Jasper ?" she asked. "No. What have you heard?" He could read in her face that she had called him to her for the purpose of imparting informa- tion. "First, Harold, tell me if you would like the way cleared to Mary Wallace's heart?" "I don't understand you. We have already decided that the chief obstruction is to be removed." "Is it thus you gauge a woman's heart? Do you fancy that the death of the hero she worships would lemove the idol from its shrine in her bosom? I have better news for you, my son." Harold at down to listen, and after urging upon him great care and circumspection his mother told him her story. The man heard and then started to his feet with an oath of exultation. "The facts have changed," he said, "and fortune smiles upon us at length." Lady Birchmear bowed her head and was silent. Perhaps she thought that the fortune which could smile upon the work in which they were engaged would prove to them as proved the head of the Gorgon in the hands of Perseus—the smile which served them would turn others to stone. But she did not speak her thoughts. She suffered Harold to depart with his convictions unshaken by such imaginings.. On the following day Lord Birchmear told his wife that he was going away on business, and that he might be gone several days. He did not tell her where he was going, and she did not ask him. She was curious enough, and would have liked right well to know his destination, but she would not trespass. She was playing her own game, and his absence would not discommode her. In another quarter, however, his lordship was not so reticent. He called upon Mary Wallace, and asked her if she wished to send any word to

her guardian. "1 am going to meet him," he said. "A courier came in last night, who reports Sir Jasper at Jungeepoor, where he met some old Minds and where he has hauled up to see a pair of tigers slot." "And you are all ready to set forth, my lord?": "Yes. What shall I tell Sir Jasper from you ?" Mary hesitated. She had it at heart to ask Sir John to take her with him. She felt the chill touch of impending evil upon her spirits; notes of warn- ing, perceptible to her inner sense, vibrated upon the dull atmosphere about her; and she would gladly have escaped from the city; but she could not ask the favour which she might have, asked had she known of the intended move at an earlier hour. So she sent to her dear guardian her love and a kiss, and the kiss she deposited upon Sir John's own lips. "I don't care to have my movements the subject of general conversation," he said, as he was about to take his leave, "so you need not mention my destination." Had he told the whole truth, he would have told her that he did not care to have Lady Birchmear and her son in immediate possession of a knowledge which might lead them to unnecessary speculation upon the possibility of a reconsdliation between himself and Arthur. That evening Lady Birchmear called upon Mary, and redeemed her promise. The premonitions of horror had not been false. . As her ladyship told her story the listener sat like one upon whom a lightning-bolt had fallen. Ter- rified and appalled, Mary Wallace heard the death. dealing words, and when there was no more to hear she sank down in a swoon. The great agony had been more than she could bear. The revelation of Lady Birchmear, guarded as it had been, and set in kindest words, had shattered the dearest hopes of her life, and rent from her bleeding heart its holiest treasure. (To be continued).

"CA.PITAL"

NotwithstAnding the fact that Lord Salsbury has gone to Dieppe for rest, a vague belief still exists in political quarters that we are on the eve of some important departure in foreign policy. It is certain that the Armenian question has reached a critical stage—so ctitical that an important movement, of the British fleet may be decided on at any moment. When a coup in foreign policy is resolved upon towards the close of a session, it is very likely Indeed to be deferred until Parliament has risenand , the Government have no longer to fear awkward questions and untimely criticism. There may he no I justification for the impression which has got abroad that something of the kini is impendtag in the present case, but it is certain that such an impres- sion exists in some usually well-informed quarters, and the newspaper despatches from the East may be worth watching tiu the course of the next week or two.

Some very beautiful , specimens of Arabic and Persian caligraphic art and some extremely brilliant examples of miniature painting on illuminated MSS. have recently been placed on exhibition in two cases in the King's Library of the British Museum. They include a large folio volume con- taining a selection of fine Hindoo miniatures and portraits, with specimens of Persian penmanship by eminent caligraphists between A.D. 1602-1739. A truly superb copy of the Koran, in seven folio volumes, written throughout in gold letters by order of one of the Mammaluke Sultans of Vgyir,,i9 among the treasures exhibited, while a fragment of the Koran, probably the eighth century, written on vellum, illustrates the earliest form of Arabic writing. The exhibition also comprises some very early scriptural MSS., among 'others, the Book of . Genesis and Exodus, according to the Syriac ver- sion, written at Amid by a deacon named John, A.D., 464, which are believed to be the earliest dated MSS. extant of any entire books of the Scripture.. A Hebrew lexicon, dated A.D. 1091, is the earliest dated Hebrew MSS. in the dopartment, while a Samaritan pentateuch dated 1356 furnishes the earliest dated Samaritan MS in the Museum. in another department where the collections illustrat- ing the various religions are on view, the section assigned to Islamism has received a valuable addi- tion in the shape of an illuminated Koran of the 16th century in original ornamented binding. It be- I longed to a branch of the Royal Family, of Delhi.

Lord Norton, who is now entertaining Mr. Glad- stone at his country house in Warwickshire, has always been far more inclined towards the Grand Old Man than to the leaders of his own party. lilB devotion to Mr. Gladstone amounts to ft sort of religion. At the time of the squabble with the House of Lords over the Franchise Bill Mr. Glad- stone sent for Lord Norton to act as his go-between with Lord Salisbury. Lori Norton is fond of telling the story of how he found Mr. Gladstone immersed in the study of his Bible on a Sunday afternoon,and yet able to look up from it at any moment, and write a note to Lord Salisbury, and then betake himself to his religious occupation again. in his speech on the kecond reading of the Rome Rule Bill Mr. Gladstone referred to a certain Tory lord whom he was proud to number among his greatest friends. This was Lord Norton.

Among living statesmsrl, Mr. Goscben writes pro- bably the worst hand. He is said to have taken up one of his own rnsmoranda the other day, and being very short-sighted, exclaimed, " A man who writes like that ought not to be in the public ser- vice!"

Much sympathy is expressed for the eleven-year- old daughter of the late M. Charles Stokes, whose .execution by an officer of,the Congo Free State has caused much excitement. She was Mr. Stoke's only child and the ohlectof his constant solicitude. It is urged in many quarters that the Congo State should pay an indemnity for the benefit of the little girl.

Railway men in the United States are still dis- cussing the "race to the north." They find it hard to accept the story of 540 miles in 528 minutes. as this would put their own record in the shade. This is held by an express which leaves New York at half-past eight, each morning. It runs to Albany, , 143 miles, in 165 minutes; thence to Syracuse, 148 miles, in 175 minutes; thence to Rochester, 60 miles, in 73 minutes; and thence to Buffalo, '69 miles, in 83 minutes. The actual speed for the dis- tance, exclusive of stops, is 52:36 miles per hour, , and on stretches where the track is suitable a speed I of 70 miles is reached.

The Press APPI‘II4 to have failed to take account of the first Irish " bull" of the new Parliament. It occurred in an admirable speech of Mr. Maurice Healey Mr. Healy was examining, with his usual knowledge and insight, the scandals of Irish Local Government. His special complaint was that the Town'Cominissioners did not possess the newer which resided in English Town 'Councils, of holding property in a corporate capacity. On the contrary, each member had to hold as a private individual, and his responsibility continued when he ceased to be a Town Commissioner. "What is worse. Mr. Lowther," continued the speaker, 4' every time he dies the same difficulty occurs:"

There is every prospect that the proposal for the abolition of the Lord Lieutenancy will get beyond the "pious opinion" stage next session. The movement in its favour on the Unionist benches is evidently very strong and genuine. One of the members who have placed motions on the subject on the paper has been canvassing a number of his fellow-Unionists upon the subject, and has,l under- stand, not met with an unfavonrable opinion. It is pretty plain now that a Unionist programme for the settlement of the Irish question is in existence, though whether it has official approval remains to be seen next session. It includes large concessions in the matter of denominational education, an im- pertent scheme of land purchase, perhaps county government, the abolition of the Lord Lieutenancy —and with it, of course, the" Castle and all its belongings—and the esteblishment of a Royal re- sidence in Ireland. In all nrobability the Unionists would like to see suer an effort made largely out- number those who favour an uncompromising at- titude, and certainly they outdo them in their in- fluence in the councils of the party. The " go- bead " men in the Unionist ranks, it is interesting to note, have great faith in Mr. Gerald Balfour. His speeches so far have been studiously concilia- tory on all matters, and although the unfortunate fact that the Irish Secretary is not in the Cabinet detracts from their weight it is naturally assumed that he does net speak without consultation with the leader of the house.

The question of crerlit to traders is being con- sidered by the chief officials of some of our railways. In the case of one of the great railways the traffic account due to it at the end of the half year ex- ceeded one million sterling—a vast sum, but • only slightly above the normal average.

The Government are, I hear, contemplating the appointment of a small committee of experts to assist Mr. Chamberlain in his design of developing and extending the resources of our colonies. „

• The world has recently been told that on one of the few occasions on which the Earl of Beaconsfield saw Mr. Chamberlain he uttered the prophecy that before he was sirty the present Colonial Minister would be a Secretary of State in a Tory Govern- ment. Whether it is from a sense of gratitude to the great prophet or merely unconscious plagiar- ism, it is certain that Mr. Chamberlain on the Treasury Bench has adopted one of " Dizzy's" most ,famous mannerisms. Heretofore the Member for Birmingham has been notable for alertness when in the House, but since his promotion the drowsiness of his demeanour has only been equalled by the promptness of his awakening when his conduct is called in question. Mr. Disraeli always slept in the House with one eye open, and Mr. Chamberlain seems bent on following his example.

THE K ‘r- Ri) Y, SEPTEMBER 7 1895.

I WARRINGTON TOWN COUNCIL.

The monthly meeting of this Town Council was held in the Council Chamber at the Town Halt on Tuesday evening. The Mayor (Councillor James Urnlough) pre- shied, and there were also present, Aldermen Gnoenall, Sutton, Hutchinson, Wright, Bolton ; Councillors Shaw Green, Smethurst, Bennett, Woods, Skelton, Hepherd, Davies, Evans, Longton, Lee, Monks, Heaton Soillcock, Shaw, Messrs. .1. L. Whittle (town clerk), J. Fairhurst (borough treasurer), and S. Wilson kassistant town clerk.)

THE RETIREMENT OF THE CHIEF CONSTABLE. The MAYOR in moving the adoption of the minutes of the Watch Committee, said they would see thAt Mr. Hunt had r.signed his position. He had been with them for nearly 30 years, and the Committee had accepted his resignation with regret, and to show the just apprecia- tion for his services, they had resolved, with the Coun- cil's permission, to him a testimonial either in coin or otherwise. He hoped the Council would see their way to agree to this, because he was perfectly certain be deserved it. (Hear, hear.) Alderman GREENALL seconded the adoption of the , minutes. Councillor DAVIES said that considering Mr. Hunt bad been with them so long, he thought the proposed testi- monial of £5O was rather paltry. It was very little more than a pound a year. If the Council proposed to give anything he should move an amendment that the sum named be increased to, say, £lOO and that it should not take the form of a money present at all. He also spoke in eulogistic terms of Mr. Hunt's services to the town. The MAYOR said the money for the testimonal could not come out of the rates.

COUneillOr BENNETT agreed with Mr. Davies as to the smallness of the amount to be given as a testimonial,and he also suggested that it was inadvisable to give £3OO a year to the new Chief Constable at first, as that was the salary which Mr. Hunt had at his retirement. Alderman SUTTON supported the proposals of the Corn- mitt-ie, and with regard to the testimonial pointed out that it was not so much a question of amount as a recog- nition of the long services of Mr. Hunt. . .

.... The MAYOR added with regard to thequestion of salary that it w.a doubtful whether they would get a good man even at £3OO. Alderman GREENALL also maintained that the salary should not be less than £3OO considering the present population and the probable increase of the borough. Councillor SHAW-GREEN spoke in terms of praise of the services which Mr. Hunt had rendered the borough. Mr. Hunt had been, he said, a thoroughly smart and efficient man who had never used his authority oppres- sively.. Councillor SKELTON suggested the opening of a testi- monial fund at the Town Hall. _

The MAYOR did not, however, agreed with this sugges- tion, but added that anybody outside could start such a fund if they liked. The minutes of the Committee were then passed. MISCELLANEOUS._ _

The minutes of the Finance and Estates, the Street Improvement, the Sanitary Works, the School Attend- ance, Cemetery, Markets, and the Water CQmnsittees, which were given in the " Examiner " last week, were all adopted without opposition. On the Street Improve- ment Committee's minutes, Councillor Davies inquired as to when a shop formerly occupied by the late Mr. Marsh at the foot of Bridge-street was going to be pulled down, but the Mayor said they could not very well pull it down at the present time owing to same financial arrangements.

LOWER THE RATES 1

Most readers will agree with me in saying, " Yes ; lower the rates; whether it be poor-rates, municipal. rates, or parochial-rates, show me the way to lower them and I will quickly adopt it ! " If this eagerness to lessen financial expense—general rates—is so universal, what must it be when the death- rate is considered? Surely no one would neglect any reasonable chance of lessening the danger of disease—to lower the death-rate. Any means; any agent ; any sug- gestion should be welcomed by all. Still; there is sur- prising apathy on the part of some on this very question. Here we have at hand, within reach of every inhabitant of every town and city in Great Britain, a means, a remedy—Warner's Safe Cure—which has been adopted and tried by thousands, where its timely use has lowered the death-rate and reduced the invalid list: but strange to say many are ailing to-day, who hesitate adopt- ing or trying this safe and sure remedy in the face of this proof ; in the face of thousands of letters from previous invalids, now enjoying good health—unim- peachable testimony of Englisk men and women ; worthy inhabitants of towns and cities from every part of Great Britain. You may ask, " How is it this remedy is so universally successful ?" Easily explained. Nine tenths of all human ailments are caused by diseased kid- neys or liver, and a sure way to cure these troubles is by treating the organs which cause them. Warner's Safe Cure acts directly upon these two organs, and by purifying the source you purify the stream—remove the cause. This medicine has now been before the public nearly twenty years, and reports from all civilized countries show it possesses the power of healing the kid- neys and invigorating the liver. Why not adopt it ? Why should precious time be wasted, and the symptoms which trouble you be allowed to increase, when friends and neighbours assure you it cured them, and in face of the abundant proof of its lowering the death rate?

HOUGHTON HORTICULTURAL SOCIETY'S ANNUAL EXHIBITION.

The weather was fine but the attendance was not as large as might have been ex- pected. IM.PE [UAL PARLIAMENT.

FRIDAY.

In the House of Commons on Friday Mr. CURZDN in- formed Mr. GREENE that the British Minister at Brussels had received an assurance from the authorities of the Congo State that all the documents relating to the ex- ecution of Mr. STOKES should be placed in his hands without delay for transmission home. Asked by Mr. DALZIEL whether the Duke of CArariamaa on his retirement would receive a pension, Mr. A. BAL- FOUR said it was not intended to make special provision from the public funds for the Duke when he relinquished the office of Commander-in-chief. The allowance His Royal Highness was entitled to under the new regulations as a field marshal was £1,300 a year, but under the old regulations he was entitled to unattached pay at the higher rate of £2,200 a year as colonel of the Grenadier Guards. When the House went into Committee of Supply on the Civil Service Estimates in replying to Dr. CLARK, Mr. Curizoia declined to enter upon a discussion of our Egyptian policy before an exhausted and depleted House. On the vote of £20,000 in connection with the cost of constructing a railway to Uganda. Mr. Cuazoir explained the view that the Government had adopted:on the matter. It weuld be absurd,he said, for the Govern- ment to undertake the protectorate of Uganda unless that country had connection with the coast. That obli- gation bad been assented to with readiness by the pre- sent Government, and the action they were now taking could be regarded as an indication that they intended to push forward this scheme with as much energy and force as were at their disposal. The Government had decided that the railway should be constructed the whole way from the ocean to the Lake Victoria Nyanza, a distance of 650 miles. The original estimated cost of £2,240,000 had been brought down to £1,865.000. The Government had resolved to do the work themselves. They had no desire to stint the line or to construct it on any other than a large and generous basis. 'I he railway would carry with it not merely civilisation and com- merce but peace, and he hoped that before the present Government left office the railway would be in daily working order running through a pacified country from the coast to the lake. After some criticism by Sir C. Drum the vote was agreed to. SATURDAY.

- -- - The House of Commons sat on Saturday, and dis- cussed the Army Estimates. On the vote fcr the War Office, Mr. BRODRICK supplemented the statement made the House of Lords by Lord LANSDOWNE with regard to the proposed reorganisation of the War Office. He an- nounced that it was intended to form a Council of National Defence under the presidency of the Lord Presi- dent of the Council (the Duke of DEVONSHIRE), and said that a detailed statement on that subject would be made by Mr. BALFOUR when he was in a position to do so. MONDAY. In the House of Commons on Monday the HOME SECRETARY promised, in reply to Sir C. DILEE, to con- sider during the recess the possibility of legislating re- garding the practice of fining and of making deductions from wages. The House took up the report stage of Committee of Supply, and upon the vote for the Admiralty offices, the First Lord of the Admiralty (Mr. GoscnEN) replied to criticisms offered on points connected with the adminis- tration of the navy. He said it was intended that matters affecting the army and navy should be consi- sidered by a Committee of the Cabinet, with the Presi- dent of the Council at the head; but there was no idea that the Secretary for War or the First Lord of the Admiralty should be put under the control of that Com- mittee. If upon a review of the general defences of the country, the Committee of the Cabinet said enough had not been done, he should bow to their decision; but if the Committee considered that less shauld be done, he should have to consider his position and responsibility to the country. It was now twenty years since he was First Lord of the Admiralty, and the extraordinary con- dition of preparedness of the country now compared with the condition twenty years ago was as astonishing as it was satisfactory. The report of the vote was agreed to. On the vote for Irish national education attention was called by several Irish members to the question of the Christian Brothers' schools. Mr. G. BALFOUR said that while he should approach the subject with the most earnest desire to include the schools of the Christian Brothers in the national system, he considered he was bound by the pledge of his predecessor that in case any rules with that view received the sanction of the Lord Lieutenant the House should have an opportunity of expressing an opinion on them before they were finally incorporated with the rules of the National Education Board.

TUESDAY.

In the House of Commons on Mr. BRODRICK, in answer to a question by Mr. Ascaorr, promised to see if any improvement could be effected in the mode of call- ing out the militia for training. Mr. Rom.Es asked for information as to how many of the sixty directorships and trusteeships of public com- panies recently held by twenty-four members of the Government had been resigned by these holders. Mr. Bar... Pone replied that he had no information at his dis- posal, nor did he know how the information could be arrived at without sending round a series of question forms to his colleagues. Mr. CURZON, in reply to Sir E. A. BARTLETT, said that documents of a voluminous nature connected with the execution of Mr. STOKEs had arrived on the previous I day, and until they were thoroughly examined it was impOssihis to say what was the nature of the charges against STOKES and under what circumstances he had been tried. Mr. BALFOUR assured Mr. Lurraxit that the investi- gation into the various proposals for making better pro- vision for the aged poor would not be by a Committee of the House. It would probably be by a Departmental Committee, and, if so, he hoped it would get to work before the next session of Parliament. I In answer to questions by Mr. BOWLES and others,Mr. Conxox said that several communications had been re- ceived from the Turkish Government with regard to I Armenia, the general purport of which had been cor- rectly stated in the press. They could not, unfortun- ately, be considered as satisfactory. The project of a new organisation for the provinces in Asia Minor in- habited by Armenians, suggested by Russia, France, and England in their Joint Note of the 11th May last, had not received official support from Germany, Austria, or Italy. It was believed they were in sympathy with it, and had unofficially advised the Porte to give it favour- able consideratirn. Her Majesty's Government were not in a position to make any declarations as to the course they mi2ht find it necessary to pursue. The HOME SECRETARY said, in reply to Lord HuGu CECIL, that he had en power to order a public inquiry into the case of Mrs. Maybrick such inquiry to be pre- sided over by a judge of the High Court and conducted 1 under judicial forms. WEDNESDAY. • • •

In the House of Com— m"—en—s---(;1—V-Jednesday, when the House went into Committee on the Indian revenue accounts, Lord G. HAMILTON made the usual statement with regard tr the Indian Budget. He said the finance of the year 1894-5 was worthy of close attention. The preparation of the figures took place at a period of great financial denrnssion. The revenue for the year was taken at Rx.so 943.500, and the expenditure was estimated at Rx. 51,245,400. But the exchange fell heavily in the earlier months of the year, and the Indian Government was compelled to cast about in order to obtaitkan equiv- alent, and to have recourse to some old taxes. It assented to cotton goods being included in the Customs duties, and from that source Rx. 354,000 had been received. There could be little doubt that the Chitral operations would be paid for out of the revenue for the year. The salt tax, which pressed heavily on the native population, had now reached its maximum amount, and he would endeavour to see if the tax could be reduced. He referred with batisfaction to the extension of the rail- way and tnlegraph systems, and said that while there was wide scope for the employment of capital in India, railway development was an essential preliminary. Mr. J. BENTON said that what the Lane ,shire manufacturers wanted was to be put on the same footing as their Indian competitors. Mr. A. J. BALFOUR said the late Secretary for India (Sir H. Fowtsta) found it necessary to impose a Customs duty in India. He laid it down in unmistak- able language that if the Customs duty should prove to be of a protective character, then the general system of taxation must be so modified by putting on a counter- vailing Excise duty that that protective character must be wholly eliminated. That gave that fair play to Lan- cashire which Lancashire asked for and he thought that both the country at large and Lancashire in particular might trust the present Secretary for India to carry out the policy which was not his only, hut which had been declared and accepted by both parties alike in that House and in this country. The usual formal resolution was agreed to, and the House adjourned.

LEAGUE OF THE CROSS.

On Sunday evening last, in the League Hall, Bewsey-street, a very successful meeting was held, presided over by Mr. John Mullins, president. Mr. Mullins announced that Sunday next the Bth inst., the feast of the Nativity of the Mother of Jesus, was the communion day of the members of the League of the Cross, and, every man was expected to be in his place at the 9 o'clock Mass. By the way (writes a correspondent) "it is peculiarly edifying and interesting to dwell on the connection of Mr. Mullins with the League of the Cross and its work. For considerably over twenty years he has been pegging away, quietly, in the most inobstru- sive way promoting, guiding, steering this temper- ance work, teaching by day and by night, not only by his most edifying life, but literally giving to some thousands their elementary and spiritual training. The catholics of Warrington, and in a more special way the members of the League of the Cross can Bever over estimate the valuable work done by this true sob, of the League of Christ's Holy Cross. The hope of all true friends of temper- ance is, that God will bless him with perennial youth and strength to enable him to persevere in all his good works."

OUR LOCAL CHURCHES-WARRINGTON.

BOLD-STREET WESLEYAN CHURCH.

The 45th anniversary of the above place of worship was held on Sunday, when special services were held in the morning, afternoon and evening. The pulpit was occupied morning and evening by the Rev. W. G. Triggs, the newly-appointed minister, who delivered two excellent sermons. The morning subject was based on Use words" For use to live as Christ," in the evening he took for his text the words, " Behold I stand at the door and knock : if any man hear my voice, and open the door, I will come into him, and will sup with him and he with me." The evening service was very im- pressive,and Mr. Triggs has already made a most favou- able impression as a preacher. In the afternoon a sacred cantata, entitled "The Miracles of Christ," composed by Mr. T. Mee Pattison, formerly of Warrington, was ren- dered by the choir, Mr. Harrison presiding at the organ. The cantata was very ably rendered, and many en- comiums were passed upon the choir by those who com- posed the auditory. Special mention should be made of the soloists, &c., who performed their duties in a manner which gave great satisfaction. Miss Jeans, Mr. Richard- son and Mr. White took the solos; Miss M. Sherlock,Miss Bennett, Miss Collin, Miss M. Kelly, Mr. Jubb, Mr. Per- kins, Mr. Oilier, and Mr. Taylor, the quartettes; and Miss Rawling and Miss Kelly, the duets. Special offer- tories were made at each service in aid trust fund.

ST. MARY'S R.C. CHURCH.

The annual sermons in aid of the Mission of St. Alban's Roman Catholic Church. Bewsey-st., were de- livered on Sunday morning and evening, by the Rev. Father Dubberrey, S.J. There were large congregations throughout the day, and the services, which were of an interesting character, were thoroughly enjoyed. Col- lections were taken at each service in aid of the day schools at St. Alban's, King-st., and St. Benedict's, Orford-lane.

LYMM.

SUPPOSED SUICIDE.—A man named Daniel North captain of the canal boats Alderney and Sark, was pro ceeding down the canal on Saturday morning when he found a dark grey serge jacket and a hard felt hat on the canal bank at Bollington, near Lymm. In the pockets of the coat were a heavy silver albert, curb pattern, a tobacco pouch, and an old pocket book,which Contained the words " Fred. Wells, tradesman, late America." The writing appeared to have been recently inserted in the book, and was written in black lead. hi- formation was given to the police, and the canal was dragged for a considerable distance, but nothing was seen until 6.30 a.m. on Tuesday morning,when Abraham Jackson, a canal banksman, saw the body of a man floating in the water near to the place where the wear- ing apparel was seen. The body was taken from the water, when it was found that the deceased had on a waistcoat which was of the same material as the coat found on the bank. The body of the deceased who is a man apparently about 35 years of age, of medium build and dark complexien, was removed to the Swan Inn, Bollinaton. to await inquest and identification. THEFT OF A SKIRT—At the Lymm Police Court, On Saturday morning before Mr. T. P. Norris and Dr. Brabszon, Sarah Ann Downwood, fustian cutter, of Lymm, was charged with having stolen a cotton skirt, value is., and 4d. in copper, the property of Ann Hemp- son, of Lymm, between the 28th and the 30th August.— Complainant stated that the prisoner had been lodging with her for a short time.and on Thurs3ay last she (com- plainant) went out at dinner time, and did not return until seven o'clock in-the evening. Prisoner had mean- while stayed in the house, but at 10 p.m. on the Thursday night she (prisoner) left and did not again return to the house. The coppers, which were in a glass bottle in a cupboard, and the skirt were then missed, the police being at once acquainted with the matter.—Sergeant Garner informed the Bench that prisoner had been 12 times convicted for drunkenness, and once for assault.—Prisoner was sent to gaol for 14 days with hard labour.

THE AMERICAN " COW PUNCHER."

" Harper's Magazine" far September contains an in- teresting article by Mr. Owen Wister on " the Evolution of the Cow-Puncher." the Mexican was, says Mr. Wister, the original cowboy,and the American improved on him. These were the days in which he was long in advance of settlers, and when he literally fought his right of way. Along the waste hundreds of miles that he had to journey, three sorts of inveterate enemies in- fested the road—the thief (the cattle-thief I mean), who was as daring as himself ;the supplanted Mexican, who hated the new encroaching northern race ; and the In- dian, whose hand was against all races but his own im- mediate tribe, and who flayed the foot of his captives, and made them walk so through the mountain passes to the fires in which he slowly burned them. Among these perils the cow-puncher took wild pleasure in existing. 'No soldier of fortune ever ventured with bolder care'emsness, no fiercer blood ever stained a border. If his raids, his triumphs, and his reverses have inspired no minstrel to sing of him who rode by the Pecos River and the hills of San Andreas, it is not so much the Ron Roy as the Walter Scott who is lacking. And the Flora Mclvor ! Alas! the stability of the clan, the blessing of the home bark ground was not there. Deeds in plenty were done that are all and more than imagination should require. For example :—One high noon upon the plains tw the Rio Grande the long irons lay hot in the fire. The young 'tattle were being branded,and the gathered herd covered the plain. Two owners claimed one animal. They talked at first quietly round the fire,then the dispute quickened. One roped the animal,throwing it to the ground to burn his mark upon it. A third came, saying the steer was his. The friends of each drew close to hear,and a claim- ant thrust his red-hot iron against the hide of the animal tied on the ground. Another seized it from him, and as they fell struggling, their adherents flung themselves upon their horses, and massing into clans, volleyed with their guns across the fire. In a few minutes fourteen riders lay dead on the plain, and the tied animal over which they had quarrelled bawled and bleated in the silence. Here is skirmishing enough for a ballad. And there was a certain tireless man in Northern New Mexico whose war'upon cattle-thieves made his life so shining a mark that he had in hank five thousand dol- lars to go to the man who killed the man who killed him. A neighbourhood where one looks so far beyond hitt own assassination as to provide a competence for his avenger is discouraging to family life, but a promisinz I field for literature. The cow-puncher is still to be found in New Mexico, his last d. main. Three things swept him away—the exhausting of the virgin pastures, the coming of the wire fence, and Mr. Armour, of Chicago, who set the price of beef to suit himself."

===== 0002601_18950907_art0045.txt | included=true | included =====
THE TRADES UNION COAGRESS.

The Trade Unions Congress was opened on Monday afternoon at Cardiff, when the Mayor, on behalf of the Corporation and inhabitants, gave a hearty welcome to ' the delegates. Mr. Holmes, chairman of the Parliament- ary Committee, announced that there were 344 duly , elected delegates present, representing upwards of one million workers. It was agreed to defer the debate on the new standing orders until next day. The rep )rt of the Parliamentary Committee was presented. Mr. John Jenkins, president of the Cardiff Trades Council, was . appointed president.

The Trade tiniens Congress resumed its sittings on Tuesday, under the presidency of Mr. Jenkins, who delivered the presidential address. Referring to the advent to power of the Conservative party, he said that, without prejudging the intentions ot the Government, the Congress should net fail to impress upon Ministers the claim of the organised workers of the country to remedial legislation. As to the Independent Labour candidates, the result of the recent elections showed that the trade-unionist voters repudiated them at the polls. Whatever might have been their intentions, the eff-ct of the Independent Labour party's campaign was to defeat candidates most in sympathy with the Labour movement and to convert the term " Labour Candidate" into a byword and a reproach. He recommended the unions to endeavour to obtain by voluntary effort an eight-hour day, feeling assured that the success of that effort would anticipate by many years the conversion of Par lament to their views. A discussion, which occupied the remainder of the day, took place on the resolution moved by Mr. J. H. Wilson, M.P., by which ttu Parliamentary Committee were censured for having, as was alleged, exceeded there instructions by making the new standing ordert applicable to the present Congress. The announce- ment by the President that voting would be by repre- sentation and not by show of hands gave rise to a scene of much excitement and contusion, and in the end Mr. ilson's resolution was declared to he lost, there has. ing voted against it a representation of 604,000, and in its favour one of 357,000—the majority against being 247,000.

At the Trade Unions Congress on Wednesday a long discussion toek place on the report of the Parliamentary Committee. A resolution was passed regretting that the Government had not seen their way to pay trads union rates of wages in all cases. Mr. Macdonald (London) proposed a resolution expressing regret that the Parlia- mentary Committee had not promoted legislation to carry out the Collectivist resolution passed by the last Congress. This was defeated by a large maj )rity. A pro- posal to strike out from the report words attributing the breaking up of the working class vote to the action of faddists "in their own body was also defeated. The report as a whole was ultimately adopted. Mr. Thorne (London) moved a resolution, which was passed, calling for the establishment by law of a general eight-hour day, mines only being excepted. A resolution calling for an amendtnent of the Employers' Liability Act so as to pre- vent contracting out and to bring all trades and indus- tries on land or sea within its provisions was unanim- ously passed, as were also others calling for the abolition , of the House of Lords and the amendment of the law of , conspiracy, and urging the Government to hold an autumn session for the special purpose of at once find- ing employment for the unemployed.

STOCKTON HEATH.

FALL FROM SCAFFOLDING.—A joiner named Norman, belonging in Latchford, met with a serious accident on Saturday at Manchester. tie was engaged on some buildings being erected by Mr. C. W. Davenport, builder, Stockton Heath, when by some means he over- balanced himself and fell from the scaffolding to the ground, sustaining severe injuries to the head. Ele was removed to the Infirmary. The scaffolding was after- wards inspected and found to be perfectly secure,' arid the mishap to 1\ orman can only be regarded as a punk accident.

APPLETON PAROCHIAL COMMITTEE. On Friday evening the monthly meeting of the Apple- ton Parochial Committee was held in the London Bridge Inn, Stockton Heath, under the chairmanship of Mr. Joseph Knowles. Tnere were also present—Messrs. W. Coppock. F. Pearson, Hugh Derbyshire, John Fledgcock, T. Hampson,John Derbyshire, Ashton Hayes,aond C. W. Davenport,with Mr. John Ashton (clerk),and Mr. William Farrington (sanitary inspector).—The Clerk read two letters with regard to the need for an improved water- supply. Mr. Robert Davies wrote thanking the Commit- tee for their attention, and saying that after the assur- ance of the Warrington Corporation he thought the re- solution of the Committee, " That no action be taken at present," was all he (Mr. Davies) could expect. The other letter was from Mr. Thomas Hall,who said that he understood the committee had decided not to rake any action because some members had been told that the Warrington Corporation intended, in the near future, to give a better supply. Continuing. Mr. Hall remarked that for five years their complaints had been laughed at by the Water Companv,and later by the memberr or the Water Committee of the Corporation. The supply was diminishing year by year, and it had now become so in- termittent and deficient that it was a mockery to call it a supply. He thought that further correspondence with the Corporatiam was a waste of time, and suggested the formulating of some scheme for a supply which might Induce the Corporation to consider it more earnes'ly,aee- ing that they would not care to lose the revenue from Appleton.—The Clerk, replying to Mr. Pearson, said the Committee would have to ask the District Council to formulate a scheme for a supply ; they (the committee) eould not do this. The Pawn Clerk of Warrington had assured them that they would endeavour to improve the supply.—Some discussion ensued, and finally it was de- cided, on the motion of Mr. Pearson, seconded ha Mr. Ashton Hayes, that the Corporation be asked what they proposed to do towards trying to improve the supply. The Clerk read a letter referring to the pollution of the river, which, he said, had bean submitted to the last meeting of the Runcorn Rural District Coancil. This letter appeared in full in the "Examiner,'and desceibed the condition of the river as simply unbearable, and,fur- ther, that the Acting Conservator of the Mersey :as be- ing communicatei with in order that the sluice might be re-opened.—The Clerk reported that in accard ince with instructions from the District Council he had written to the Town Clerk that the Rural District Coun- cil hardly liked at present to enter into the question of joining with his authority in undertaking to construct the sewer for taking the sewage from Stockton Heath into the borough sewers.but urged him to press upon the Manchester Ship Canal Co.or the Conservator of the Mer- sey the necesaity for re-opening the sluices under the new road at Wilderspool, as they were advised that if this were done the nuisance would be to a great extant abated.—lt was resolved that for the present no furth2r action be taken in the matter.—Mr. Pearson said that As the nights were now drawing in, the question of more efficiently lighting the Grappenhall-road ought to be considered. It was very necessary that more lamps should be placed in the road. After some further discuaa skin, Mr. Pearson moved that the Corporation be re.• quested to light tha lamps on the same terms as before, and that Messrs. Davenport and Hampson meet the officials of the Corporation and point out where the lamps should be fixed.—On the.minutes being brought before the meeting of the Ruecorn Rural Council on Tuesday afternoon, Mr. Baxter, C.C., referred to the state of most of the new streets at Stockton Heath, and, said it was hardly possible to go up them.—The minutes were passed.

A boat accident by which three lives were lost is re- ported from Cemaes, a small watering-place on the Ang- lesey coast, near Arrdwch., On Wednesday afternoon an open sailing boat in charge of two coastguardsmen was- in the act of jibbing when it was capsized by a F.uddett gust of wind, and the occupants, seven in number, were thrown into the water. Twoladies who were amongst the passengers were rescued, snd the coastgusrdsmen also escaped with their lives; but the other three—two ladies and a gentleman, all from Liverpool—were drowned,

RUNCORN AND WIDNES.

VISIT OF THE REV. J. PULFORD WILLIAMS. At St. John's Presbyterian Church on Sunday.,sermons werepreached by the Rev. J. Pultord Williams, formerly pastor of the church, but now of Cardiff. There were large congregations, and satisfactory collections weie taken on behalf of the building fund. THE NORTHWICH CONTEST.—CANDIDATES EXPENSES. A return has been issued by Mr. Hugh Lyle Smythe, high sheriff and returning officer, of the election ex- penses of the candidates for the Northwich division at the last election. Sir John T. Brunner's expenses amounted to £1,290, and Mr. Ward's totalled £1,125.

BETHESDA CONGREGATIONAL CHOIR. The Rev. J. Simpson, pastor of Bethesda Congrega- tional Church, again occupied the pulpit on Sunday after a short holiday, and preached the annual choir sermons. There were good congregations at both services, and the collections were satisfactory. The anthem, "In Jewry is God known," was sung at the morning service by the choir, and in the evening, "Behold how good and joyful," and "Magnify and glorify," were sung. fwo solos were given, the lane by Mr. E. Worrell being "Star of Bethlehem," and that by Miss Heatherington "Rock of Ages," to a new musical arrangement. Mr.C.LeCouteur presided with his usual ability at the organ. SERIOUS BURNING ACCIDENT AT RUNCORN. A girl, 12 years of age, named Mary Ann O'Neil living with her parents in Back Water-st., sustained serious burning injuries on Monday morning. It appears that she was engaged in lighting the fire, when a burning stick fell out of the grate. In attempting to put this out with her foot, her clothes became ignited, and before the flames could be extinguished her clothes were des- troyed, and almost the whole of her bodyseriously burnt. Mr. Frank Speakman, of High-st., rendered first aid. In the meantime a messenger was sent for Dr. Robinson, and that gentleman found the girl burnt almost from head to loot. She lies in a serious condition.

ST. PAUL'S CHOIR SIMMONS. The Rev. G. de Putron Gliddon, the newly-appointed minister, preached the anniversary sermons on behalf of St. Paul's choir, on Sunday. There were large congrega- tions. The discourses were highly appreciated. On each occasion the choir rendered special musical items. In the morning, they rendered McCartney's " Te Deum " and the anthem " 0 how amiable are Thy ways" ; and In the evening they sang the anthem "I will give thanks" ( Elvey) and "Gloria in Excelsis " (Mozart), whilst Miss Alice Taylor gave an effective rendition of the solo from "Elijah," " I will sing of Thv great mercies." The efforts of the choir reflected credit upon themselves and their conductor, Mr. R. Morris. Toe organ was presided at by Mr. Grine. The collections on behalf of the choir fnnds realised £l2 7s. _

_ THE LATE MRS. BAILEY.

The funeral ofMrs. Bazley took place in Runcorn Cemetery on Sunday. Amongst those who joined in the procession were the Rev. W. George, Messrs. P. Speak- man, J.P., C.C., T. Boote, sen., J. Parker, A. linsworth, J. L. Littlemore, J. Hayes, R. Peacock, W. Tickle, and J. Rowe. These were followed by members of the con- gregation worshipping in Camden Chapel, members of the Camden Mission Band, and members of the de- ceased's society class. The bearers were Messrs. Lambert Hayes, Rathbone, Meredith, Millineer, Dean, Woodward, and Goodier. In the chapel the Rev. W. George, atter reading the burial service, gave a brief address, in which he alluded to the kind disposition of the departed one. Those present then sang" Rock of Ages." At the grave side, after the remaining portion of the service had been conducted, the hymn " Jesu, lover of my soul" was sung. On the coffin were many beautiful floral tributes, forwarded by Mr. Speakman, Mr. J. L. Littlemore, Mr. and Mrs. J. H. Green, Mr. and Mrs. L. Woodward, Mrs. Bennett, Mrs. Rathbone, Mr. and Mrs. I. W. Temperley, Mrs. and Miss Temperley. Mrs. Lambert, Mrs. Millinger, and Miss Rossbottom. of Sutton.

RUNCORN CYCLE CLUB.

The annual 25 mile race was successfully run off on Wednesday over a good course—starting from High Legh to Cranage and back. Fourteen entered for the race but only nine started. The result was first, S. Webster, 15 minutes start ; second, W. Rushton, 25 minutes start ; third, W. H. Hill, 25 minutes start. The net times were G. Whenyates 1 hour, 24 minutes.fastest time medal ; C. Johnson. 1 hour 26 minutes ;W. H, Mack 1 hour 31 minutes 19 seconds ; W. Grounds 1 hour 31 minutes 20 seconds ; S. Webster 1 hour 31 minutes 55 seconds ; W. Rushton, 1 hour 42 minutes 1 second ; W. H. Hill, 1 hour 44 seconds. The times are considered good when the strong cross wind blowing, going-out and coming in, is accounted for. The winners' pace was about 14 miles an hour. second and third, 14 miles an hour, fourth and fifth, 17 miles an hour, scratch men.— Whenvates and Johnson, 18 miles an hour. The starter was Mr. W. Wiswall.checker at Cranage,Mr. H. Hughes, judges Messrs. Wiswall. Powell, and Smith. The first, prize consisted of a gold medal, second, silver medal gold centre, third, silver medal. The gold medal tir fastest time was given by Mr. G. Shone.
