RUNCORN RUGBY FOOTBALL CLUB..

The formation of the Northern Football League has resulted, as was anticipated, in practically forcing the Runcorn club into the league. To remain outside,would have meant almost ruin to the members of the Runcorn club inasmuch as it would have robbed them of no fewer than a dozen of their most important fixtures. This would have been a most serious matter as the " gates " would have been considerably lessened in consequence of theleague matches at Widnes proving a greater at- trartien. Accordingly it was decided to join the league if possible, and a deputation waited upon the committee appointed,which met at Manchester on Tuesday evening. The application of the Runcorn club wan accepted, and ' the list of fixtures was drawn up. The first match is to be played to-day (Saturday), at Runcorn, when Widnes will be their opponents. As the clubs forming the league are the best in the North of England, some good matches should be witnessed during the season.—The association club also commence their season to-day, when Warring- ton will oppose them. RUNCORN TONIC SOL-FA CHOIR. There was a good attendance of members at the annual general meeting of the Runcorn Tonic Sol-fa Choir,held in Ellesmere-st. schoolroom on Monday even- ing. In the absence of Mr. P. Speakman, J.P., C.C.,the chair was occupied by Mr. T. F. Borrows. The financial statement was submitted by Mr. Newall from which it appeared that the session commenced with an adverse balance of upwards of £7, and concluded with a credit balance of El 1.1. a. Mr. Wesley Cooke, the sezretary. then submitted his report which stated that there had been a large increase in the number of honorary mem- bers, and also an encouraging increase in the singing members. The average attendance at the prac- tices was in advance of last year The report also re- ferred to the success of the choir at the Crystal Palace. Both reports were considered highly satisfactory and were adnpted.—Mr. P. Speakman was unanimously re- elected president, and the vice-presidents were elected en bloc. Mr. Holford, the energetic and painstaking conductnr,was again chosen to that position. Mr. Cooke h A virg expressed a desire to retire from the secretariat, Mr. J. W. Yates was appointed in his stead, and Mr. Wilson Newell was elected assistant secretary. The fol- lowing were also appointed :—Treasurer, Mr. William Coventry ; librarian, Mr. Brown ; awl organist, Mr. William Cooke. The election of committee and con- sideration of several other matters in connection with the work of the choir were postponed. The usual votes of thanks to the officers for their services having been passed, a harmonious meeting terminated.

SUDDEN DEATH OF A WOMAN.

Mr. J. E. Wordey held an inquest at the Royal Hotel, Runcorn, on Monday morning on the body of Elizabeth Nutt, 43 years of age, who died suddenly on Saturday morning.—Mrs. Naylor was the first witness called and stated that her mother had suffered from a cough for about twelve months, but was not medically attended. Witness was sleeping downstairs on Friday night in the same room with the deceased, and about five o'clock on Saturday morning she was roused by her mother who requested her to fetch help as she was dying. She called John Threlfall, a lodger, and then went for medical aid, but Dr. McDougall said he had a very important appointment,the case requiring his attention being a serious one. On witness returning home within half-an-hour she found her mother dead, having expired in Threlfall's arms. On the previous afternoon Dr. McDougall was in the house but her motker was then in good health and did not require his attention. The deceased had not been drunk on the Friday night but she had taken •‘ tidy "drink in her life.—John Threlfall, boatman, stated that the deceased appeared to be in good health on Friday evening and was quite sober. He gave evidence as to being called on Sattirdly morning tc deceased, and to the fact that she died in his arms within about ten minutes.—Dr. Mc- Dougall considered death was due to rupture of a blood vessel,probably of the lungs. He had heard remarks as to her intemperate habits, but he had always found her a steady and decent woman.—A verdict in accordance with the medical testimony was returned. THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, SEPTEMBER 7, 1895.

RUNCO RN TECHNICAL INSTITUTE. OPENING CEREMONY. DESCRIPTION OF THE BUILDING.

Sir John T. Brunner, M.P., visited Runcorn on Satur- day for the purpose of performing the opening ceremony of the Technical Institute. Every effort was put forth to make the event a memorable one in the history of Runcorn, and the inhabitants showed a considerable amount of enthusiasm in the affair. The question of the provision of a technical institute is one which has occupied the minds of many who are interested in the welfare of Runcorn for some years and at this point it may not be unappropriate to trace the history of the movement. Just about live years ago,at a town's meet- ing, a committee was appointed to consider what use might be made of recent legislative enactments for the beriefit of the town. In December, 1898, the committee reported to the Runcorn Improvement Commissioners (the local authority under the Technical Instruction Act) that public education in Runcorn virtually ended with the elementary schools, and that the new Acts would, if taken advantage of, be of the greatest service in making provision for something higher. The Run- corn Improvement Commissioners thereupon, supported in their Action by this committee—a large and very re- presentative body—determined not to let the oppor- tunity go by. They instructed Mr. John Bowden, CE., to prepare plans for a building suited to be the perma- nent home of higher education ; they made application for building grants to the Science and Art Department, as well as to the Cheshire County Council, and, finding public opinion with them, called in the aid of a local rate of one penny, the limit placed upon their powers by the Legislature. The appeals for help were not made in vain. The Science and Art Department gate a grant of £l,OOO towards the project, the County Council £440, and Sir John T. Brunner, M.P , started la subscription list with £5OO. With such assurances the Committee felt justified in pushing for- war.4 the project, and decided to proceed with the ern- Lion of the building. A site in Waterloo-road, in close contiguity to the Town Hall, was decided upon, and April 14th, 1891. saw the foundation stone laid by Sir John Brunner. The entire work of erection and furnish- ing has cost about £4,500. Unfortunately the promoters hate not succeeded in opening the building free from debt as they at first anticipated, but this may be ac- counted for in some measure by the depression in trade. The adverse balance exceeds £1,500, but, efforts to con- siderably reduce this will be made shortly. The building itself, as already stated, has been built to plans drawn up by Mr. John Bowden, C.E , F.S./., of Manchester, and Mr. Wilding, the assist- ant surveyor, acted on behalf of the Council. The principal elevation fronts Waterloo-road, and is built of Ruabon bricks with stone dressing. There are no pretensions to architectural beauty the main consideration being the general utility of the building. On entering the ground floor we find one large classroom admirably fitted up with desks and chairs, a rostrum for the teacher and a pedestal and blackboard. With regard to the desks it may be stated that they have been so constructed as to be interchangeable. A separ- ate desk may be provided for each student, or if desired the top part may be removed and the lower portion utilized for upholding a longer desk. Another advantage is that by a most simple but ingenious arrangement the desk may be used in either a horizontal or sloping posi- tion. The blackboards too, are the first to be used with the present arrangement. The blackboard consists of two parts and they are so fitted up, that when the lower portion has been written on,it may by a simple arrange- ment pushed upwards and the other portion will take its place. Passing from this room we come to the practical room which is also on the basement. It is so fitted up that it may be used either for practical electricity or practical plumbing. The heating room and lavatories are also on this floor. With regard to the heating it may be stated that it is on the low pressure system, and is connected with a vertical extracting shaft as the main outlet. Proceeding to the ground floor we enter the corridor,nine feet wide,which is well lighted. A notice- able feature of the rooms on this floor is a very fine art room arranged Be that freehand, model,and perspective can be carried on at tho same time. It is well-fitted up, exceedingly well lighted and ventilated. This room may also be used for lantern lectures. The lec- ture room for machine and building construc- tion and similar subjects is at the rear of the building on the same floor. It is fitted on the same principle as the classroom described in the basement. It is divided from the practical room by a small teacher's room. The practical room is provided with Trainor's patent benches and a chest of lockers for stowing away the work of the students during the ses- sion. It is also fitted with furniture and other appliances to enable the teacher to demonstrate the various subjects taken. On this floor there is also a model room which is fitted with cupboards with glass doors, running along both sides and a broad shelf under the windows. 1 large number of models will be kept here. A directors' room for committee meetings ke., and an office adjoins this. Mounting the stone staircase we reach the first floor and this is largely occupied by a fine large room which is de- voted to advanced art. Numerous life-sized models are mounted on pedestals and paintings displayed on easels, and the room is lighted by a large unobstructed window extendin.! 18 feet from the floor, in order that perfect lighting of the models and other objects may be obtained. Near to is the laboratory fitted up for eighteen element- ary and eighteen advanced pupils. The fittings for this are most complete, each student being provided with every convenience necessary in this branch of study. The same remark applies to the compounding room which adjoins it. The theatre for theoretical chemistry, alkali manufacture, etc., is a well arranged one. The lecturer's desk is provided with water, gas, and other necessaries for demonstration lectures. Proceeding to the other end of the building on the same floor, a room for quite a different purpose is provided, viz., cookery. There are special arrangements for gas and coal stoves which are of a most improved principle. The lec- turer's table is of special design, giving the teacher good facilities for demonstrating before the stu- dents. Another model room and lavatory complete the number of rooms on this floor. The whole of the furni- ture, which is all removable, has been designed by Mr. Wilding, and the improvements which lie has intro- duced do him infinite credit. In fact, the Institute will stand in the front rank as one of the most completely arranged institutes in England. Messrs. John Heywood and Sons, of Manchester, have supplied the fur- nishing,and Mr. James Mort,of Greenway-road,Runcorn, has fitted up the office. The builders were Messrs. A. and H. Hulse, of Winsforti, and Mr. Edwin Hatton, of Manchester, has had in hand the lighting arrangements. As one advantage of the Institute, it may be stated that a number of very fine models which have been in the possession of the town, and which could not before be seen through lack of a building are now on view. The work of students during past sessions have been framed and are exhibited on the walls. The Technical Instruc- tion Cemmitree of the Urban District Council have al- ready engaged 17 teachers to undertake classes in the various sciences, art technology, commercial subjects, and domestic economy. A syllabus giving full particulars is now to be obtained. THE PROCESSION.

Saturday's proceedings were commenced by a luncheon which was held at one o'clock in Greenway-road Primi- tive Methodist Schoolroom. To this the nress were not invited. A most imposing procession of Volunteers, the composing the Trades, Friendly, and Temperance Societies together with those connected with the St. John Ambulance Association (Runcorn branch), Fire Brigade, Urban District Councillors, officials and others, was held at three o'clock, and paraded the principal thoroughfares of the town. The route taken was along Lowland-road, Shaw-street, down Greenway-road,Fligh- street and Bridge-street. to Mill House, Halton-road, re- turning by way of Bridge-st.,and Waterloo-road,to the In- stitute. Mr. G. H. Kirkham satisfactorily filled the duties of marshal!. The Volunteer band was at the head of the procession, and played lively tunes under the sonductor- ship of Bandmaster Outhwaite. The Runcorn regiments of the 2nd Earl of Chester Volunteers followed under the command of Captain G. Ashton. Other officers pre- sent included quartermaster Sergeant Hankinson, Colour Sergeants Mort and Poole. The trades societies were headed by the members of the Runcorn branch of the Associated Shipwrights Association, and a fine show they made. Over 100 were present and they wore a bunch of oak leaves as buttonholes, and some carried models of boats whilst others displayed several tools used in the trade. Amongst those in the procecsion may be mentioned Mr. H. Dale (secretary), Mr. Taylor (trea- surer), Mr. W. Littler (master shipwright) and Mr. Evans (foreman shipwright). The Boiler MakerS, ac- companied by Messrs. Nathaniel Lightfoot (prealient), William news (treasurer) and W. Mottershead (steward) followed next. They were succeeded by about 30 joiners among whom were Mr. W. B. Cropper (steward) and Mr. J. Williams (treasurer). THE OPENING OF THE INSTITUTE.

On arriving at the Technical Institute, the Volunteers formed in line, and as Sir John passed between,the band struck up the tune" Fine Old English Gentleman," and the concourse that had assembled gave vent to their feelings by hearty cheers. Mr. unison, in presenting Sir John with a silver key, with which to open the building, said his duty that day was very simple, and be could assure them very agreeable. Be did not intend to take up their time with any history or description of the Insti• tute, and would not anticipate or interfere with any ad- dresses they would shortly hear in connection with it. But he might be allowed to say that it was a great satis- faction to every member of the council, and he had no doubt to every citizen who had taken an interest in the , movetnent,to find that the Institute was now practically finished, and would in a few days take its place as one of the educational institutions in the town. (Applause.) He sincerely hoped that when the classes were opened the youth of the town would avail themselves of the oppor- tunities whereby they might acquire the skill which would enable them to fight more cleverly and easily the battle of life. (Hear,hear,and applause.) It was a ter- ble disappointment to see hundreds of children leaving their elementary schools fairly well educated, and then Co find them drifting into the hopeless mass of humanity which were continually crowding the market of unskilled labour. That institution was intended to remedy that state of things, and he was sure that Sir John Brunner and other gentlemen who had assisted to build that in- stitution would think it their greatest reward if the young people took advantage to make themselves com- petent to earn their living in a much easier way. With- out further remark he would simply, on behalf of the Runcorn Urban District Council, ask Sir John Brunner, who had been a sincere friend and generous supporter of that movement, to accept the key of the building.and to declare the Institute open. He then handed to the hon. gentleman a silver key. Sir John Brunner having opened the principal door, declared the building open, and consecrated to the people of Runcorn for all time to come. (Loud applause.) On re-appearing on the lawn in front of the Institute, aftea an inspection of the building, Mr. F. Wiswall (chairman of the Technical Instruction Committee)presented him with a beautifully illuminated address in album form, and it was read as follows by Mr. Frederick A. Lake, the law clerk : —" The Urban District Council of Runcorn desire to offer you, on the occasion of the oper ing of their Technical Institute,their sincere gratitude and warmest thanks for the mun'fieence and sympathy you have extended in connection with the establishment of this institute. As the first and largest subscriber, you give an impetus to the project, and by your practical sympathy have encouraged this council in their work of providing a suitable building for the carrying on of technical instructien As a member of Parliament you assisted in the passing of the Technical Instruction Acts, and by your services in connection with university education,free public libraries, and liter- ary and scientific lectures have placed the inhabitants of this district under lasting obligation to you, and which is freely acknowledged. They believe you will witness ' with pleasure the c repletion of this institute, knowing as you do that the industrial progress of this district and the proficiency of its workers is largely dependent on facilities being provided for a thorough technical train- ing. The assistance you have tendered to elementary and universal education encourage them to hope that you will continue your efforts in the direction of obtaining an Intermediate Education Act,as they believe that such an Act is urgently required,and they would welcome the day that this inctiwte can be utilised for such an invalu- able purpose. The opening day will mark an important step in the development of this town, in whose moral, social, and intellectual welfare you have manifested so deep an interest." (Laud applause.) In making the presentation Mr. Wiswall eoid they all hoped Sir John would live long and enjoy good health. It was only a email taken of their feeling towards him, and they were under great obligations to him for what ho bad done for Runcorn. They wished him, Lady Brunner, and his family every prosperity in this world. (Loud applause). Sir John Brunner, who on rising, received an ovation, said he was very proud to stand before them that day to witness with them the end of a good work and the beginning of a better work. (Hear, hear.) He was very much struck with the thoroughly appropriate, character of the words that had been used in addressing him by Mr. Wiswall, who had done himself and those present honour in the way in which he had led them in the work they were engaged in celebrating. Mr. Wiswall was good enough to express a hope that he should live to enjoy the work that they were doing that day. He did most thoroughly enjoy that work, and Mr. Wiswall could net have touched him more dearly than by wishing that be should ,Sojoy this work for the future. He was not going to follow the flattering words used in the address read by Mr. Lake ; suffice it to say that the kindness dis- played towards him by the Urban District Ccuncil of Runcorn was a further encouragement—following upon many and many acts of a like character on their part —to him to continue to devote his life, health, and strength in the great work of helping forward the educ- ation of tie people of this country. (Applause.) They were opening that day a noble home for a noble insti- don. They were giving to the young people of the town opportut3ties and means for living henceforward fuller, happier, and brighter lives than they had had the I chance of living up to now. They were doing their best to abolish that frame of mind which had been described as the blues or megrims,or vapour—they wereabolishing that emptiness of mind, or something towards it, of which the two extremes might be found in the loafer who propped up the public-house by his shoulder, and the man so wittily described by Gilbert as the " Greenery - yallery, Grosvenor Gallery, foot in the grave young man." (Laughter.) They Were doing work which would fill with a greater

and higher interest the lives of the young people of Run- corn and be was happy to be aiding in it. (Applanse.) He heartily wished success to the Technical Instruction Committee, to the teachers who would assemble within the Institute, and to the students who would come in large and increasing numbers day by day. What was it that those teachers were going to offer to them? They were going to offer to them as their eolleagues were throughout the country—for there was not a county that was not doing its part in that work—the means for securing for England that commercial and manufactur- ing supremacy, which they as patriots ought to aim at. (Applause.) That the work was needed required no words for him to prove. They were all happily con- vinced that England needed technical instruction for the working men, boys and girls. He had an illustration of j the truth of this only a few days ago from a friend who told him of a 'deputation sent on the Continent by the iron and steel trades of Great Britain, composed partly of employers and employed, in order that they should see for themselves why it was that the iron and steel trades of various countries on the Continent were more advanced and more prosperous titan the corresponding trades in this country. He was not going to prophesy what the report would be which would be presented to the members of the institution named, but he kne v that a large portion of the deputation had been impressed with the fact that it was not altogether on account of low wages and long hours the men on the Continent worked, but it was because the English manufacturers were behind the Continental manufacturers in methods and plant. In the Institute the teachers would, he hoped,so influence the minds and fingers of the students who came to learn,that in days to come the young men who left the building to go out into the world would use improved methods and plant in the various trades they entered, that we should cast behind us once for all what was considered a shame to them as Britons, that they were behind any nation in the world. (Applause.) They should hold that up for themselves, and remember that they had in the Institute an oppor- tunity for their children to obtain the information to beat the manufacturers and merchants of the Continent and the United States, if only they would take full advantage of the teaching given there. If so they would set England at the bead of the world in matters of that kind, as she oughtro be. (Applause.) A philosophical friend of his told him that they were beginning at the wrong end ; they ought to be tryieg not to educate the workman, but the employer. But he claimed it was the duty of the State and the community to help those who needed assistance, and not to spend their money on those who otherwise could afford to obtain those advantages. He asked them to be convinced from that day that that I great building and the great organisation of the County of Chester, behind it,with the organization of the Science i and Art Department, and the Education Department I 11,.hind that, that the work was done not for the sake of I wealthy manufacturers and merchants, who could afford ' to send their boys and girls to the most costly institu- tions, but it was intended for the mass of the people, and there was no one so lowly in Runcorn who should not feel that the Institute was his own, and that he had a right in it, and that he would see that sa far as his household was concerned, full advantage would be taken of it. He might say a good deal about the advantages that were to be had at the Institute for their girls as well as boys. He started with his friends in VVinnington a cookery class not long ago, and they ' found there,es was perhaps the case in Runcorn,that the housewives declared they knew very well how to cook I all they could get to cook. (Laughter.) But from the I mothers they appealed to the fathers,who thought it a I very good thing that the girls should be taught to cook, I and after the first lesson there were in the corridor a number of happy, eager looking fathers, anxious to buy the dishes the lasses had cooked. (Laughter.) He did not know whether it was necessary to appeal to the I mothers or Runcorn to use their influence in getting the I girls to learn laundry work, dressmaking, and cookery. I But if they failed he asked the fathers to intercede for their daughters,and see that they attended in large num- bers. Runcorn had done well in a good many matters I in times gone by, and he hoped it would take pattern by ; them, and would secure for the Technical Institute a 1 long and splendid career of prosperity. (Applause.) 1 Runcorn was behind in some matters. They had a small proportion of scholars taking advantage • of the benefits I offered by the County Council, but he hoped his words , would induce a good many to do so. He was not going to use the common clap-trap and say that if only one ' came int that school as a result of his words he would be satisfied ; he wanted that school filled, and he would be proud if the rooms were filled from end to end and on every floor with an eager, earnest and enthusiastic band of students. (Ap- plause.) He was delighted to hear that in the last few days greater and greater interest was shown in the pros- pect of the work to be done in the Technical Institute, ; and he would adds few words more as an encourage- ment to them, in order to see what might be obtained by constant and painstaking attendances at those classes. I There were two young men engaged in the Crewe rail- way works full time, leaving their homes; at 5.30 a.m, and returning at 6 p.m., who attended the Mechanics' Institute there, took advantage of the County Council lectures, and who won scholarships. Both went to the Technical Schools in Manchester, took up a scientific subject necessary to their future proeress—mineralney— and both as a result of their earnest and strenuous work in Crewe, continued in Manchester, followed their suc- cess by securing a Whitworth scholarship of £5O a year, Another instance was that of Miss Hall, of Macclesfie'd She had done marvellously well, and he was satisfied that if all the young people he saw before him entered into the contest with determination, there might be ' among them such bright examples as those from Crewe, and also another Lucy Hall, who left Macclesfield, hav- ing won a County Council scholar,hip, and as a result of her continued studies at London University had secured the Tu free! scholarship, which would enable her to con- tinue her studies in the research of the science of chemis- try He trusted he had said enough of the work being done by the Technical Instruction Committee to urge upon them the great advantages to be derived from the in- struction imparted, and if he had been successful in this as the Runcorn Technical Instruction Committee had been successful—and nobly successful in theirs, and it led them to utilize the means provided, he would look back upon that day as one of the brightest and happiest days of his life. (Loud applause.) Dr.Flewitt, C.C., stated that he had been asked on be- half of the County Council to express the pleasure it gave to the members thereof to see that noble and magnificent structure open for use. They were aware that if the County Council of Cheshire had done no other good work than to administer carefully the £12,000 per year placed in their hands by the wonderful fluke of the beer duties, they would have deserved well of their country- men. They had had no small difficulty,for although the Council had been unanimous so far as concerned voting upon the question, they had a numSer of silent sceptics who were only to be heard of in dark corners, when meetings were not held. But there were those who said they might have used the money to reduce the rates. But fortunately they had a number of gentlemen ready to devote it to the purpose of education, knowing th., ad- vantages that would be derived therefrom. Those who had had faith in human nature believed that the Anglo- Saxon had not lost his power to conquer in the civil field. He was sure they would use the instrument aright, when put into their hands,and it would be their fault if they did not participate in the advantages offered them. He trusted that the workingman would utilize it to the fullest possible extent. This kind of education when de- veloped from below would reach the upper ranks. When the artizans made then-selves felt, the other members of .society would be quickened to go before them, and the result would be that they would develop the interests of both. As members of the Technical Instruc- tion Committee they were highly satisfied, for they had the best accounts of those Ipossessing these scholarships. They had every rear an account of each student read out to them, and in every instance, from Liverpool, Warrington, Manchester, and London, they had nothing but encomiums respecting students going from their, county. He had great plea- sure in congratulating the Chairman of the Runcorn Urban District Council and Sir John Brunner on the completion of the work. Mr. Speakman had some time ago said he was in a bad and hopeless way about the pro- vision of that building, but he had kept working on un- til the Institute was finished. He wished to congratu- late them upon the completion of the excellent work. (applause.) The Rev. Canon Wood said that it was with the greatest possible pleasure that he moved a voteof thanks to Sir John Brunner. Be did this because ho recognised In Sir John Brunner a thorough believer in the advant- ages of education. He knew that in every way in which Sir John had been able, he had encouraged !those who had endeavoured to press forward education in that town. He was the only subscriber, he thought, to their Free Library. He helped them very much when in diffi- culties about the Oxford Universities Extension Lec- tures. He forwarded presents to the School Boarchwith- out being asked, for the benefit of the scholars in the town. He also sent them one of the finest magic lan- terns, and now he had done a great deal by making it posssible for the town to have that institution. He thought he might safely say if Sir John had not encour- aged those who thought of it in the most substantial manner, they would nt have possessed that institute. He was not at all ignorant of the fact that everyone did not appreciate the Institute. It would take too long to tell them of the advantages afforded by the building. He read on the previous night an article on the very curious subject of cockroaches, and among other things was the question, "What do cockroaches eat ?" (Laughter.) The answer was, "What do cockroaches not eat 2" Now what did that Technical Institute teach ?" He was not going to answer it by asking what did it not teach ? It would teach them about their own houses. The housing of the people was the greatest of all, but if they noticed the syllabus they would see the construction of houses, and almost everything connected therewith, as well as their habitation, and even going so far as to deal with the dresses of the ladies inhabiting them would be dealt with.. Then there would be everything that could tend to make the work done in Runcorn useful and first class. Among other things was soap-making, and he hadn't a doubt that some foture Runcorn lad would succeed in throwing Pear's soap into the shade, and something far better than Sunlight. (Laughter.) They were going to teach alkali manufacture. Sir John had told them that

we were going to beat foreign countries, but they at Runcorn were going to beat Brunner, Mond and Co. (Laughter.) If they could do that they would be exceed- ingly fortunate. (Laughter.) He thought that every- body present would forgive him if he stepped from the subject of the day, and spoke of the time when Runcorn was in great necessity through the terrible distress which prevailed last winter; and he did not suppose that they had seen the end of it. He knew that within the last two weeks two respectable married women had died owing tu the privation which they and their families went through when living "hard "last winter. Now, there was no one who had helped them like Sir John, and they were aware he need not have done it. People sometimes said sneeringly that a man who had been blessed by God could afford it ; but they knew only too well that many men had a great deal of money,but had not the heart to dispense it. They heartily thanked Sir John because he put his hand in his pocket, and because he had done so with the best possible intentions. It was really a pleasure to stand ' upon a platform wherethere were no politics and no re- ligious distinctions ; where all united to push forward and do what they could for the town. The place would only succeed by the enterprise and perseverance of the ' people, and they would be greatly assisted by the pro- vision of the Technical Institute. The two good things ' must go together,and therefore it was for them to make • the best use of the new building. He was sorry to say it i was still in debt to the tune of £1,500. He did feel very grateful to Sir John Brunner for what he had done, for they in Runcorn were not used to generosity on the part ( of the richer people. He heartily wished they were. He ' had been grieved to find that if they wanted anything done they had not to go to the richer people, but to the poor, and therefore they heartily thanked him. (Ap- - plause.) . _ _

. . Mr. Speakman said he had the greatest possible plei. sure in seconding the motion so ably moved by Canon Wood. It was not necessary for him to recapitulate the benefits to be derived from using the advantages pre- sented by the Institute. He might add for their en- couragement that Runcorn had five boys receiving the benefits of what the Cheshire County .Council Technical Committee had done, and he hoped one if not more of them would go to Lmdon and bring honours back. He was very glad to tell them that the previous day the Technical Instruction Committee gave a grant of £313 annually to help to sustain that institution, which with the penny rate would mean £513. Then the County Council had given them £5OO towards the building of I that institution.

Mr. F. J. Norman, J.P., was delighted to say ditto to all that had fallen from Canon Wood and Mr. Speak- man. The possession of wealth brought in its train splendid opportunities and tremendous obligations. Be need not explain to them that Sir John Brunner did possess the wealth, did enjoy to the full its advantages, and happily for Runcorn did not shirk its responsinilt- ties: (Applause.). . .

The 'Cli;irman;in putting the motion, said he wished to concur with what had been stated. The resolution was carried with three hearty cheers. Sir John Brunner, in responding,said he had been very much touched by the words used by Canon Wood,and he would ask to be excused from dwelling upon what he had said. In a parting word or two he might tell thcm how impressed he had been with the unanimity shown on that platform, a unanimity he was happy to say which had existed from beginning to end of the work of the Technical Instruction Committee, the Urban District Council,and those whom they had called in to assist in their labours, which had culminated that day. They had men of various religions,different occupations, and all shades of politics.

Mr. Speakman proposed a hearty vote of thanks to the Chairman which was carried with enthusiasm. Sir John and Lady Brunner were heartily cheered as they left the lawn. A number of persons afterwards took the oppor- tunity of inspecting the Institute. AN EVENING CONCERT.

In one of the art rooms of the Institute a concert was given in the evening. 'rhere was a very large attend- ance. Mr. E. Imison, J.P., occupied the chair, and in the course of a few remarks urged upon his hearers the necessity of doing their utmost to make that Institute a useful institution in the town. The concert was of a mast interesting and entertaining character. The pro- , gramme commenced with a valse " L'Ardita," by Mr. R. Berkett Musgrove which he played with musicianly taste and skill. A glee party consisting of Misses A. ylor and Simk in, Messrs. Lancaster and Branch very successfully contributed a couple of glees. Mr. Hugh Montgomery ot Mancnester, infused much merriment into the proceedings by his humorous contributions, and was encored thrice. The soloists were Miss A. Taylor, Miss Sitnkin. and Mepsrs. IL Morris, F. L. Scott, P. Bate (Warrington), H. Branch, J. Booth, and Atherton. All sang in good style, and encores were frequent. The Miss.s Taylor and Simkin also sang a duet, 'Go, pretty rose." A vnte of thanks was passed to all who had taken part, on the motion of the Chairman, seconded by Mr. H. J. Grice, and a pha.sant evening's entertainment concluded.

RUNCORN CO-OPERATIVE SOCIETY,

MUTED.

Another meeting for the revision of the rules of the above-named society was held in the Co-operative Hall, Runcorn, on Tuesday evening. Mr. Sales again sccu- pied the chair, and there was a good attendance. Mr. G. Millington said he thought it was necessary to have a list of persons entitled to vote. At present they did not know who was entitled to vote.—Mr. Watson said a register would be provided at the polling booth when the election of committee took place.— Mr. Millington said his difficulty was that under rule 2 any person who made a deposit of l.s. and signed the declaration was a member, and they might make any number of bogus members in that way.—Mr. Ashton said a member was held responsible for three shares.—Mr. Slade proposed and Mr. Poulson seconded that no member should be allowed to vote who was not a member for six months. —Mr. W. Millington moved an amendment that the period of qualification be three months.—Mr. Leadstone seconded.—Mr. James Johnson and Mr. George Rutter having been appointed tollers,it was put tolhe meeting and the amendment was carried.

Rule 13, relatimg to management was then proceeded with. The first paragraph having been read,Mr.Poulson moved an amondment that the committee should be divided into two sections of six each. The first six to take charge of the business at Bur,corn,and the others to have charge of the business at Widnes,and sit at Ditton- road, Widnes. Both committees to sit at the same time and be connected by telephone. Also that the two committees meet as a joint committee at the registered offices once a month. His object in doing so was to fix the responsibility of every department on somebody's shoulders.—Mr. Dudley said he had had the opportunity of going against Mr.Poulson on this matter previously and the only change he had made was in the matter of the telephonic communica- tion. S(eing that there was no seconder he would make a proposition that the paragraph remain as at present.— Mr. Eden seconded.—lt was decided that the first para- graph should remain as at present. With regard to paragraph 2, Mr. Slade moved that it be adopted with the alteration that eight instead of five of the committtee should form a q ioruin.—Mr. Ashton seconded, and it was carried.

On the motion of Mr. Dudley, seconded by Mr. Pick- that' it was decided that no person should be eligible for the committee who had not been a member for more than twelve months.—Mr. Poulson moved and Mr. Ashton seconded that no person should he eligible who did not hold 10 share, in the society.—Mr. Millington moved as an amendment that no member should be ' eligible for the committee unless he held three fully paid up shares in the society, and to hold them six months before the meeting, and to hold them whilst in office.— Mr. Leadstone seconded.—Mr. Poulson withdrew his proposition, and the amendment was carried. Mr. Jabez Stubbs moved that no person should be eligible for the committee who had a "blood relation " working for the society.—Mr. G. Millington said they must not overlook the fact that the society employed over 100 people, and the relations would be debarred from taking any prominent part in the management of the society.—Mr.Dudley spoke in favour of making some alteration in that direction. He said it was a fact that some partiality had been shown, and that where a com- mittee man's son was employed in a shop, the manager had his hands tied. When there was dissatisfaction amongst the employes it was detrimental to the best in. interests of the society.—Mr. Fitzpatrick and Mr. Poul- spoke in support of the proposition.—Mr. G. Millington said he quite agreed that something of that kind was re- quired,but he advised them not to go to extremes. After turther discussion the proposition was carried. Mr. T. J. Holt stated that it had been proposed that three of the committee should retire each half-year. Be (Mr. Holt) was in favour of a constant change on the committee, and he thought no member should be al- lowed to serve more than four years in succession.— Mr. Rogerson said that he would be going to the trouble of educating a man in the business cf the society for nothing.—After further discussion Mr. Holt's proposition was rejected. Mr. Poulson moved the adjournment of the business to Widnes on Tuesday next.—Mr. Moulton seconded, and stated that they should do as they would be done by. The Widnes members had come to Runcorn for two or three meetings, and it was now the turn of the Runcorn mein- , bers to go there.—Mr. Fitzpatrick thanked the last ' speaker for his remarks. The resolution was withdrawn until rules 14, 15, 16, and 17 were passed without altera- tion, and it was then careied. A notice was handed to the Chairman asking him to 5 Call a special meeting at Widnes for the purpose of con- sidering toe action of the committee with respect to Mr. I Thomas Riley's election.

The Catholic priest at Freidheim, Germany, has died suddenly while delivering his sermon after celebrating mass. It is supposed that the wine used at the celebra- tion had been poisoned.

A lad named Butterworth, of Castleton,n.ar Heywood. who was bitten by a. do:raoyeral weeks ago,has now died in terrible agony from hydrophobia.

BEFORE THE MAGISTRATES.

THE ALLEGED BREAKING OF k WOMAN'S ARM BY I VIOLENCE. At the Warrington Bor.ugh Police Court on Friday, Mary Gleeson, Dutton-St, was charged with assaulting Catherine Robinson, Dutton-St., on the 9th August and also with inflicting grievous bodily harm on the same date. There was a cross summons against Robinson by Gleeson for assault at the same time.—Mr. Browne had appeared for the plaintiff, but stated that as it had been proved to him that her arm was not broken, thouoh put in splints, he withdrew from the case.—Mr. Davies defended, and called Dr. Bowden from the Infirmary, wno stated that the arm was not broken but only con- tused. He (Mr. Davies) contended that Mrs. Robinson charged Mrs. Gleeson with making false statements and went to her house to argue it out. She there forced her way in and broke two vases. Mrs. Gleeson had to for- cibly eject her. Mrs. Robinson denied entering the house and called two witnesses who supports I this statement, and added that they saw Mrs. Gleeson strike complain- ant with a brush upon the arm. Complainant said she had been unable to use the arm since.—Mr. Davies said complainant was seen by SO or 60 people to enter defend- dant's house and after the blow, with her so-called broken arm, to thump at the locked door, and clap her hands.—The Bench came to the conclusion that there were serious falsehoods on both sides, and dismissed the case.

A MUSICAL PASSENGER. At the Warrington Borough Court on Monday,William G. Grier, who was stated to belong to Belfast, was charged with being drunk and disorderly at the Central Station, and also with assaulting some railway passen- gers at five minutes to seven on Saturday night. Samuel Fernsey, a railway policeman,said that the prisoner was taken out of the 6.55 p.m. train from Liverpool on Saturday night in a drunken condition, the other pas- sengers having to hold him down on the carriage floor. He was given in charge of P S. Rowbottom and taken to the polite station.—The prisoner said that he was going from Manchester to Belfast, and on the way he wanted to sing but the rest of the passengers did not seem to be pleased and they held him down on the floor, and had given him a wound near the eve.—A representative from the station said that he could not he going to Bel- fast as he was going towards Manchester. The prisoner however adhered to his statement, and P.S. Rowbottom said that a ticket collector on the station had identified the prisoner as being one from whom he had collected a ticket in the Liverpool train earlier in the afternoon so that it appeared as though the prisoner must have gone to Liverpool and being either drunk or asleep, had come back again. The prisoner was ultimately fined 2s. 6d. including costs. THIRSTY TRAVELLERS. Patrick McGrath and James Govan, both belonging to Newton-le-Willows, were each charged at the Warring- ton Berntigh Court, on Monday, with being drunk and disorderly in Winwick-read, and with bre-111,m,, a square of glass in the window of the Horse and Jockey Inn, on Sunday Morning.—Peers Brimelovv, the landlord of the Horse and Jockey, said that at 20 minutes to 5 on Sun- day morning, the two prisoners awoke him by knocking at the door. On going down to see what they wanted they said that they had come from Chester and wanted a drink. Witness told them they ' had better go to Chester to get a drink, and he then left them. They were partly drunk, and soon afterwards witness heard the window break. P.C. Lewis was near by, and he went after them, and the two prisoners were 10C1sed up—P.C. Lewis gave corroborative evidence.— The prisoners admitted calling at the Horse and Jockey for a drink but denied that they broke the window. The magistrates decide i to fine the prisoners 53. including costs, and also ordered them to pay the cost of the win- dow.

CIIMIGFq OF CRUELTY TO RonsEs. Joseph Taylor, 82 Catherine-street was charged at the Borough Court on Monday with working his horse while man unfit state on August 22nd. P.C. !Inlet) said that the man was, on the date mentioned, driving the horse attached to a lorry laden with a large quantity of water pipes. The horse was swaying from one side to the other, and appeared to be in great pain. Witness or- dered him to take the horse out of the lorry and take it home again.—lnspector Loff R.S.P.C. A. said that the horse was suffering from virrhus cord and was incurable. It had since been killed.—The bench inflicted a fine of 5,. and coste.—A charge of a similar offence alleged to have been committed on AuguQt 24th, by John Walker, Old Hall, Stretton, was adjourned fora month.

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, SEPTEMBER 7, 1895.

WARRINGTON COUNTY PETTY SESSIONS. WEDNESDAY.—Before Messrs. W. C. Jones (chairman), Mr. Z. Armitage, Mr. W. Mortimer, and Rev. G. F.

RAberts. DRUNKENNESS —Emma Clarke, Sankey, and Alexander Flannigan, Sankey, were fined for this offence. DOG AT LARGE.—Richard Walters was fined ss. for allowing his dog to be at large on August 22, at Sankey

Mr. Jenkins: DOO3 he deal very largely with von? Witness: Be is a rather good teistomer.—Answering the Chairman, he said that during the past two or three years the number of persona who visited Croft was about the same. There had been no apparent increase. George Brimelow, who had assiszed Mr. Mains at the bar of the Horse Shoe, testified to customers to large numbers of visitors frequenting the heuse, especially on Sundays. Some of them asked for spirits, but of course could not be served. The house ought to have a wine and spirit licence ; it was situwed amongst the dwellings of the villagers, and he thought it would be an accom- modation to these persons as well as the visitors if they could get spirits at the Horse Shoe, instead of having to go to the General Elliott, or the Joiners' Arms. The na- tives visited Mr. mains' house.—Cross-examined by Mr. Hockin,he said the General Elliott and the Joiners Arms might be able to cater for the requirements of the public, but he thought after a man had spent his money in a place he should have a share in the trade. The Chairman We want to know if the licence is re- quired. We cannot deal with the question of dividing the trade of a place. By Mr. Knowles: Witness. as an old villager.could say that if one Man had all the trade of the village he would not grow fat upon it. (Loud laueliter.) At this juncture the Bench intimated that they had heard enough evidence in favour of the applicant, and expressed a desire to hear what the eppesing, solicitors had to urge against it. Mr. Jenkins asked to he allowed to eall jest one mere witness, namely, Joshua Ball, farmer, of Croft.—The Bench acceded. This witness testified to the resvetability of the appli- cant, but could not speak as to the requirements of the neig,hbourhcod.—ln cross-examination he remarked that it was his opinion that there were sufficient licensed houses in the villa.-' so far as concerned the villagers. At times, when fêtes were held, there was a little diffi- culty in catering. Mr. Hockin contended that the only evidence that had been adduced in favour of the application was as to the respectability of Mr. MAini. Tne evidence of some ot the witness.,3 was actually in favour of the opposition. For instance the Rev. Rector had said that the village did not need the license,and alter this, he (Mr. Hockinl had quite expected that Mr. Jenkins would have ac- cepted the situation, and have withdrawn the applica- tion. Mr. Heckin went on to eentend that the present facilities were ample, and denied that the landlord of the General Elliott had ever had to turn away a single customer because of overcrowding. He then sugeested that the Superintendent should he asked to go at once into the w)tness-box, and that if he said that the license was not needed,the magistrates should dismiss the appli- caticn. After a short consultation between the justices this course was adopted. Superintendent Hieeinbottom was then sworn, and said th,t he had been in the locality for five years. and was of opinion that there was ample accommodation for all persona wha either lived there or visited the place. During the p-st three or four years the visiting public had decreased in numbers rather than increased. . The magistrates witheut retiring, decided to refuse the application. Juett proved the case and a fine of 54, was imposed. NOT USING THE REINS.—John Stewart, a carter of 11, Sutherland-st ,Runcern, was charged with having on the 26th August unlawfully driven a horse and waggon with- out reins. l'.C. Richardson' whe proved the case, stated th it the reins w-re fastened to the collar. The defendant was sitting on the cart. He was fined ss. and 9s. 61. costs. THEFT BY DT/S.—Enoch Shaw (10), John Bowden (13). Thomas Gresty (14), and Stephen Rose (11), were all charged with stealing a pair of opera glassros,c,ne hall of twine, one oilitone, with a quantity of goods includ- ing butter, bacon, matches, tobacco, value Hs. 3d., the goods of John Daniel Shaw,' from the cabin of the flat Herbert at Runcorn, on the 31st August.—The captain (John Shaw, jun.), William Antrobus, watchman, of Robert-street, and James Mitchell, Stanley-street, also gave evidence as to seeing the lads about the flat on the date named and also to capturing three of them. They found tobacco and matches in their pockets.—P.C. Wylie stated that on being questioned the two prisoners acknowledged to haying entered the flat, and they had also some of the stolen property with them. Be brought them to the Police Station and charged them with the offence. They admitted that one was as had as the other, and that there was another lad named Thomas Gresty with them at the time. The following morning he visited the flat in company with Captain Shaw and found that the cabin had been completely ransacked. The door of the cap- tain's private compartment had been forced open and a quantity of articles taken, and placed an a seat outside ready to he removed. He brought all the articles down to the police station. Eva had been thrown about in the cabin and it had been left in a disgraceful condition. —P.C. Gregory stated that he apprehended the lad Gresty, on Sunday morning. On seeing witness the lad ran down Back Arthur-street, but witness succeeded in overtaking him. He at first denied having been with the lads, hut afterwards stated that he had only taken some tobacco, about two yards long, and he threw it into the canal. lie went on the flat to get something to eat—Superintendent Atherton stated that all the prisoners had been previously before the magis- trates. Shaw and Gresty were" polished thieves." The latter had no parents in the town to look after him.— Mr. Norman, who had taken Mr. Handley's place in the chair, said the magistrates hardly knew what to do with the lads. They were a pest to the police officers and everyone else. Grestv would be sent to Knutsford gaol for fourteen days and afterwards to a reformatory. Bowden was ordered to receive twelve strokes with the birch-rod, and tlie other two, on account of their age,six strokes each.—The Chairman asked the parents to look after the lads more carefully in the future.

HOWLEY-LANE BAND OF HOPE. The children attending the Howley-lane Band of I Hope, numbering upwards of 250, partook of tea in the Temperance Hall,llowley-lane,on Tuesday even- ing, the tea being provided by Mr. John Naylor, of Beeston Towers. The arrangements had beep made by Messrs. W. H. Pearce. George James, and W Roberts, and gave every satisfaction,and the tables were presided over by a number of the young ladies who attend the Band of !lope. The catering was in the hands of Mr. John Toft, of Bridge-St. After the tables had been removed a concert was given, a large number of the parents and friends of the chil- dren attending. An excellent miscellaneous pro- gramme had been arranged by Mr. James,the secre- tary. Mr. John Naylor presided. The choir, com- posed of a number of boys and girls attending the society and several of the workmen at Howley Mills, sang " Temperance boys and girls" and "Me beacon light with good effect. Mr. E. L. Pearce ;sang "Before I had taken the pledge "in grand style, and together with his sister,Miss Mabel Pearce, was equally successful in two pianoforte and violin solos. The remainder of the programme was made up of recitations, dialogues and songs by he children. A mrtAt enjoyable evening was brought o a close about 10 p.m. YORKSHIRE V. LIVERPOOL AND DISTRICT WATER

POLO M ATCH. A water polo match, Liverpool and District V. Yorkshire was played in the open air lake at the Winter Gardens, Southport, on Saturday, before about 500 spec-ator4. Teams :—Liverpool and District: C. Morris (Everton), goal ; S. Beaman (Warrington) and W. Williams (Diamond T.), backs; J.F. inegan (Sefton). half back ;T. Locker (Warrington), T. Ripley (St. Helens), and J.Formby ( Everton), forwards. Yorkshire: S. Thompson (Bradford), goal ; S. Womersley (Batley), .T. Wood- head (Wakefield), backs ; J. Wingfield (Hunslet), half back ; H. Thornton (Bradford), W. Lodge (captatn, Wakefield), and W. Fothergill, forwards, The Lancashire men won the toss,and took the end with the sun at their backs. Blinded by the sun. the Yorkshire custodian soon let the ball roll through, but the score was equalized within five minutes. Many fouls occurred, but the shiny sur- face of the water exposed to the brilliant eun,hine made it impossible for the spectators to concen- trate their gaze on the lake for many seconds at a time. The play continued pretty even, but neither goal keeper when at at the bright end could see the ball, and the goals there registered were of a fluky character, Eventually the Red Rose representa- tives won by four goals to two, the players finding the water very cold. Mr. A. E. Birch (captain of the Southport Swimming Club) was referee. A flying squadron race across the lake and back was well contested, Liverpool winning by 16 seconds.

NEWTON PETTY SESSIONS.

ANNUAL LICENSING SESSIONS. The annual licensing sessions for the Warrington County Petty Sessional Division took place at the Court House, Newton-le-Willows, on Saturday morning, before Messrs. T. J. UillespiO (in the chair), R. Stone, C.B. F. Borron, S.S. Brown, and Colonel Wood. SUPERINTENDENTS' REPORTS. Superintendent George Brassington's report was as follows :—" Within the townships of Golborne ond Hay- dock, which form the Wiean p-irtion of Warrington petty sessional division, are 32 licenseersons, viz.: 13 innkeepers, one spirit grocer, and 18 ..leersellers (13 on land five off the premises), being the same as compared with last year •, 117 persons have been proceeded against for drunkenness and drunk and disorderly con- duct,being an increase of 46 as compared with last year. There i 9 one application for a license to keep a billiard and bagatelle table at the Sir Charles Napier beerhouse, Gelb( rue. The houses, generally speakidg, have been well-conducted, and I have no objection to make to the renewal of any of the licences." He assigned as the principal reason for the increase in drunkenness the colliers' strike in 1893.—Superin- tendent J. flig,ginbottom, of the Leigh section of the division, reported as follows :—The number of licensed persons is the same as last year : Licensed victuallers, 45 ; beer-sellers (on premises), 16; beer-sellers (off prem.. 6es), five ; grocers and wine licenses, two ; total, 63. During the year one licensed victualler had been con- , victed. There is one new appl,cation for a license—that of Isaac Mains, landlord of the Horse Shoe Inn, beer- house, Croft, who applies for a licensed victualler's license. The comparative return of persons licensed and proceeded against for drunkenness shows an increase of 49 proceedings for drunkmness compared with last year. This increase I consider is principally due to the colliers' strike in 1893, is the number in 1894 was the lowest for nine years. Generally the licensed persons have con- ducted their houses satisfactorily, and there have been

few complaints. The in4p;istrates inquired if there were any objections to any of the old licenses bein2 renewed. Superintendent Brassington and Iligginbrittorn intim- ated that they had no objections to raise to the

renewals. The Bench then renewed the licenses held in both divisions. TRANSFICR OF A HAYDOCK PUBLIC-TIOUSE. Harriet Alice Tunstall applied for the transfer of the license of the Huntsman Inn, ilaydock, lately held by her husband, now deceased.—There was no objection made, and the application was granted. APPLICATION FOR THE RE-BUILDING OF IN INN. Mr. T. Garner. solicitor, submitted to the Be ch n plan tor the proposed re-building of the Ram's Head, Haydock. The owners of the house were Messrs. Greenall, Whitley and Co., who had been centernolating certain alterations to the premises since 1889. Various plans had been prepared, so that the convenience and requirements of customers might be better attended to. The conclusion, however, which the owners had come to was that the alterations set forth upon the various plans would not, if carried out, prove satisfactory, but that the best course would be to re-build the house entirely One additional room would be made, the " snug " in the old building would be dispensed with, and the commer- cial-room would be made somewhat smaller, so that a lavatory might he provided. The plan he had handed up to the Bench would show their worships that two additional bedrooms would be erected, a bathroom, and a private staircase, &c. There would also be a distinct and second bar for the outdoor trade ; there would be ventilating sheaves in each of the public rooms,whilst a new drainage scheme would be laid down to the satisfaetion of the local administrative authority. The Flaydeck Urban District Council had approved of the plan, after having made certain suggestions which would be carried out to their satisfaction. He hoped the magistrates would likewise express their approval of the plan. The Chairman drew the attention of Mr. Garner to the fact that access could be gained to the back yard of the house through a door near to the second-class bar. They did not think it advisable to have back entrances. Mr. Garner observed that any suggestions which came from their Worships would be attended to. The Chairman asked Mr. Garner if, supposing the justices approved of the plans, his clients would consent to the closing of the back door leading into the yard should the police see any necessity for it.. Mr. Garner replied that he thought they would con- sent. The Chairman then said that they had no objection to the plans except what they had already referred to. AN APPLICATION REFUSED. Mr. Jenkins made an application on behalf of Mr. Isaac Mains, landlord of the Horse Shoe Inn, Croft, for a full licence te enable him to retail wines and spirits. His client had occupied the Horse Shoe Inn as a beer- house for 22 years. during which time no objection had been made as to the conduct of his house either by the police or the public. He had steadily effected improve- ments in the premises for the convenience of his cus- tomers and the development of his trade. Only five years ago he had caused to be erected a large dining- room for the accommodation of visitors from a distance who resorted to his house to hold parties, &o. Mr. Mains would do anything reasonable, which the Bench might deem desirable, to further improve the premises. A number of the principal inhabitants of the township would be called as witnesses to support the application, and in addition to thae his client had obtained the ap- proval of the Rector of the parish and also of the Roman Catholic priest. He had likewise a petition in favour of the applieatien, signed by several of the ratepayers. Mr. J. W. Knowles, who appeared on behalf of Samuel Daxon, the landlord of the Joiners' Arm's, Croft; and Joseph Vi'histon, grocer of the Same locality, for the put, pose of opposing the application, said he understned it was the practice of that court not to accept petitions of this kind, and he, thet4ore, objectel to Mr. Jenkins making mention of memorials or petitions. The Magistrates' Clerk upheld Mr. Knowles' objection, whereupon Mr. Jenkins remarked that he would drop it Paying that he had plently of material to support his case with- out handing in the petition. His cUts t '.i premises were in every way suitable to meet all requirements that would be made upon them if he obtained the license to sell wines and spirits. It was now a matter of great in- convenience to persons visiting the Horse Shoe Inn that they could not there obtain spirits and wines. Mr. Mains had frequently been asked for them, but could not supply such needs, and his customers had conse- quently to leave him and go elsewhere. /fader all the circumstances he though the application was a very reasonable one. William Walker spoke to having prepared the plans which had been handed up to the magistrates for their inspectim.—Cross-examitted he said the nearest fully- , licensed house was the Joiners' Arms, 242 yards away ; the next was the General Elliott, 429 yards distance Isaac Mains, the Applicant, gave testimony corrobor- ating Mr. Jenkins' opening statement. He had purchased the Horse Shoe Inn in 1880, and had sine, laid out a ctn. siderable sum of money in improving it. Large numbers of visiters from all the country round patronised his house, and inconvenience often occurred through his not possessing a spirit license.—Crom-examined by Mr. Heckle, the selinitor for the landlord of the General Elliott and who likewise opposed theapplication, witness answered that it was true the residents and the travel- I ling Public could procure wines and spirits at the General Elliott. The population of the rewnship had not Increased to his knowledge since 1880. In 1891 the estimated population was 914. He could not say that there was now more difficulty in obtaining spirits than II e i. The Rev. Henry Isaac Wadesen, rector if Croft, testis fled to baying known the applicant for some time. Hi.. family was a most respectable one, and the applicant was also a man of much respectability. He knew the Horse Shoe Inn, and considered that Mr. Mains was quite fitted to hold a full licence. He would conduct , the house quite as respectably as any other person I would.—Cress-examined, the rev, gentleman said he did not consider that the granting of a. spirit licence to this house would improve the village, neither was he ' aware that there was any need for this licence —Cross- eve mined also by Mrs Knowles, the Rev. H. I. Wadeson replied that a great many people visited the village of Croft on Sundays. Mr. Knowles :—Do you as a Chrietian man and a parson suffer very much inconvenience from the visits of these people ? The Rector :—I think they are a great drawback to the place.—Replying further to Mr. Knowle's question, wit ness said bethought there were plenty of public-house- in Croft already. Samuel Thomason, farmer, asserted that the applicans was a respectable man, a, d that the full license wa- very necessary.—Cross-examined by Mr. Knowles, wits ness said he had entered the Horse Shoe Inn thrice that year; he had never suffered inconvenience. Reginald Owen. an overseer of the parish, also thought the Horse Shoe Inn should have a full license. He had very often heard persons express a desire for spirits while in the hence, and it was decidedly objectionable to hie mind, that customers shmilel have to leave one house for another because they mild not get what they desired.—Cress-examined by Mr. Hortrin : The present state of things had existed for many years.—Replring to the Chairman, witness said the General Elliot was often overcrowded in consequence of the Horse Shoe net hav- ing a full license.--In further cross-examination witness admitted that he was a brewer, but had nothing what- ever to do with the General Elliott. Mr, Jenkins (to witness): Have you anything what- ever to do with Mr. Mains' house? Witness : No, sir. Mr. Jenkins: He does not purchase from you a larger quantity of beer than most of your other customer 3 ? Witness: No sir; I wish be purchased more. (Laughter.)

NEWTON URBAN DISTRICT COUNCIL.

The monthly meeting of this Council was held at the Earlestown Town Hall on Monday afternoon. Mr. C. B. F. Borron,J.P., C.C., presided, and there were also pre- sent Messrs. T. Ormand, W. Gilmour, P. MacMahors, J. Simpson,J. Smith, J.P., J. Slee, E. T. Evans, W.Thomp- -83/1, jun., W. Beatty, F. J. Smith, Rev. E. W. W. Kaye, J. E. Woreley (law clerk), and R. Brierley (clerk and

surveyor). - THE LATE COLONEL MCCORQIIODALE. The Law Clerk said that in conformity with the desire of the Council at the last meeting he had forwarded a copy of the resolution passed under the common seal of the Council to Mrs. McCorquodale at her residence at Gadlys.—The Chairman said he had received a letter from Mrs. McCorquodale thanking the Council for their expression of sympath7.—On the motion of Mr. Smith, seconded by Mr. Ormand, it was decided to place the _

letter on tlie minutes. HIGHWAYS, ETC., COMMITTEE. The minutes of this committee which we published last week were passed unanimously on the motion of the Chairman, seconded by Mr. Thompson. GAS AND WATER COMMITTEE—MR. MeCMARON REQUIRES AN EXPLANATION. . - .

The minutes of this committee which we published in our last issue contained a long correspondence between the committee and Mr. F. W. Webb, of Crowe,with re- gard to the water supply. Their confirmation was moved by Mr. Smith and seconded by Mr. Simpson.—Mr. Mac- Mahon objected to the minutes referring to the corres- pondence of the sub-committee on the water question. He wanted an explanation of that correspondence, and he wished to resign his position on the sub- committee.—The Chairman : You are out of order. It will be very well to make a personal explanation as to the reason of your resigning your position after the minutes have been dealt with.—Mr.MacMahon contended that he was in order. He had a right to ask for an explanation.— Mr. Gilmour asked at what time in the sub-committee's proceedings did they take Mr. MacMahon into their con- fidence as a member of the sub-committee.—Mr. Smith said that he was not aware that Mr. MacMahon had not been taken into confidence.—Mr. MacMahon : I ask you on whose authority was the correspondence of March 20 passed between Mr. Webb and the sub-committee ap- pointed to act. During the time I have been appointed on that committee I have not been summoned.—Mr. Smith: The correspondence was made on the authority of the sub-committee.—Mr. MacMahon : It has not. have not been acquainted with it. lam a member and yet I have not been summoned.—Mr. Gilmour : I have not yet had an answer to my question. I demand an answer.—The Chairman : I rule you out of order.—Mr. Gilmour: I wish to have an answer to my question.— Mr. MacMahon : And I have not yet had any explana- tion of the conduct of the committee.—Mr. Gilmour (to Mr. Smith) : At what stage was Mr. MacMahon ac- quainted with the proceedings ?—Mr.Smitli : My answer is, I cannot answer you.—Mr. MacMahon : As long as you carry on hole and corner meetings you will not be able to answer.—The Council then divided on the minutes, and for them,there voted the Chairman,Messrs. Wood, Siropson, Smith, Thompson, Slee, and Kave-7 ; against, Messrs. MacMahon, Gilmour, Beatty, and Or- mand-4. The minutes were therefore carried. bCHOOL ATTENDANCE COMMITTEE.—CHARGES AGAINST A scßoot,. Mr. V nod moved, and Mr. Smith seconded, the adop- tion oft 's committee's minutes—Mr. MacMahon said that he m ticed that certain charges made by the school attendanc • officer against a certain school were not on the mime os. He moved that they be added.—Mr. Beatty seconded.—Mr. MacMahon (continuing) said that Mr. Ball had come before the committee with a charge against the Havdock schoel that two children had been sent back from the school because they had not their pence. This ought to have been put upon the minutes and ought to be investigated. He characterised Mr. Ball's statement as a unfounded charge.—The Chairman said that it would be bettor to refer the matter to the committee.—Mr. MacMahon accordingly moved this with the proviso that the charge be put upon the minutes of the next meeting. He would ratlva• move a vote of censure on the school attendance officer on the ground that he was not a fit and proper person for his p (" Oh, oh").—Mr. Smith thought that the matter had better be adjourned. He believed he would be able to put the matter in such a light as would satisfy the members.—Mr. Batty, as a manager of the school in question, said that Mr. Ball's statement was altogether unfounded.—Mr. Ormand said that the matter had been referred to the committee to look into and examine the charges made by Mr. Ball. He thought that the matter could be brought before the special committee to report upon.—The Clerk: Your amendment, Mr. MacMahon, is that Mr. Ball's report be referred back to the committee. —Mr. MacMahon : I want the charge put upon the minutes. Mr. MacMahon then agreed to the proposed amendment, and Mr. Beatty seconded and it was carried.—The minutes of the committee were then carried.

TECHNICAL INSTRUCTION COMMITTEE. The minutes of this committee which appeared in our last issue were passed on the motion of the Rev. E. W. W. Kaye, seconded by Mr. Thompson. A LETTER FROM FATHER LEEMAN. _ WARRINGTON BOARD OF GUARDIANS.

SPECIAL MEETING. A special meetine of the above Board was held in the Workhouse on IN ednesnay, under the presidency of Mr. William Houghton, to consider the recommendation of a sub committee of the Kitchen Repairs Committee that the tender of Messrs. Bradford, of Manchester, for the supply of a new boiler and other additions and improve- ments to the conking apparatus for the sum of £238, exclusive of brick work, be accented. The attendance also included—Messrs. S. Davies (senior vice-chairman) and G. Pilling (junior vice-chairman), the Revs. W. Moore, T. R. Matthews. and J. H. Riddette ; Messrs. J. Billinge, J. Clarke, J. Dienum, J. Houghton, W. Hick- man, T. Halton, A. Jolley, A. J. Jolley, A. Lang, T. B. Malkin. G. Thompson, and J. Warburton, with Messrs. J C. Sutton (clerk), S. Sutton (assistant clerk), C. H. Ward (master), and H. M. Mines (superintendent Pad- gate Schools). The ASSISTANT CLERK in reading the minutes produced a letter of resignation from Nurse Lockhart, who said that the great strain she had borne had rendered her un- fit to go further without some rest. She theretore re- signed in order to get that rest which was so desirable. A letter, written just before the meeting of the Board, further explained that Nurse Lockhart had tendered her resignaticn not because she did not care for the position —on the contrary, she was wishful of retaining it. She, however, understood that it was against the rules of the Board to grant an holiday to anyone who had not been in their employ twelve months, and had thought that there was no alternative hut to resign in aroer to gain a short rest. She would he willing to withdraw her resig- nation if the Guardians would allow her a short leave of absence. Mr. CLARKE moved that the minutes and recommenda- tions of the Kitchen Repairs Committee be accepted. In referring to the sum proposed to be spent in erecting new boiler, kn., he remarked that the sum of £2.38 would perhaps seem rather large to the members of the Board,but it had happened rather unfortunately for them that the cooking apparatus had broken down at the same time as the boiler, which made it seem like double ex- pense. The committee now recommended that the ap- paratus for cooking and baking should be divided. They had previously been joined, which perhaps had a tendency towards wearing out the cooking portion sooner than it might have been had they been divided. The committee had found that they had no alternative but to replace the old apparatus by new. They had carefully considered the estimates on Saturday, and had decided to adopt that one tendered by Messrs. Bradford. There would be, no doubt, a saving of fuel effected, which would possibly recoup them for the extra outlay. With regard to the resignation of Nurse Lockhart, he thought it ought to stand. It was a rule, as she understood, that they did not erant holidays to those who had not been in their employ for twelve months, and she ought not to be allowed to play fast and loose with the Board like this. Before folks sent in their resignations they should first satisfy themselves whether they intended to leave or not. (Hear,hear.)—Mr. A. J. JOLLEY seconded.—Mr. Tnosrasox suggested that Nurse Lockhart be brought before them to give her reasons more fully.—The CHAIRMAN : She has already explained them in her two letters. I think it would be rather unusual to act as you suggest -- The CLERK intimated that if the whole of the minutes of the committee were approved it would really constitute an acceptance of Nurse Lockhart's resignation which was contained in the first letter. The second letter had only been written that morning, and was not, of course, included in the minutes. He advised the Board simply to deal with the minutes only as far as concerned the repairs to the boiler, Sze.—Mr. CLARKE remarked that with the consent of his seconder he would amend his motion and exclude the resignation of Nurse Lockhart. —Mr. ía. J. JOLLEY assented.—The CLERIC observed that the matter relating to the nurse related properly to the House Committee, who would consider it at their next meeting. —The minutes were then put as amended, and were carried, the Clerk being instructed to prepare a contract, and to submit the same for the pproval and seal of the Board.
