THE THEFTS FROM MESSRS. SKELTON'S

SALE.

COMMITTAL OF THE PRISONER.

Last Friday, at the Warrington Borough Police Court, before Alderman Wright and Mr. J. 11. Fleming, William Bolton, 33, York-s'., Latchford. was charged with steal- ing from the warehouse of Messrs. Skelton and Ca., sometime between August 14th and 22nd, a large and miscellaneous quantity of goods, value £l3 193. 6d. Mr. Davies prosecuted for the Company,and Mr. Browne defended. Mr. D ivies, in opening the case, said the prisoner was well known in Warrington, and had been employed and trusted by Mr. Lowe, the auctioneer as door-keeper. For this work he received 18s. per week. He commenced his duties on Thursday, the 14th August, and from that date until the 22nd, he (Mr. Davies) was sorry to tell them that almost daily he pilfered from the rooms in one way or another; altogether lie took 400 articles consisting of brushes, lamp, revolver, enamelled pans,fire brasses,and over a cwt. of stair-rods. ' ete.—Mr.Browne had said the stair-rods were purchased f roir a third party at the sale and were taken to Mr. B3lton's house. It seemed to him,Mr. Davies, that it had been a premeditated robbery for the reason that even before he stole the goods the prisoner went to a number of parties and told them what goods were in the shop for sale and explained that he could get the articles cheaper than anyone else, and in consequence some of them gave him orders. Mr. Russell received a number of brushes from the prisoner and paid 2s. 6th for them in good faith. When he ordered them they were not stolen but Bolton immediately and deliberately went down to the shop and stole the brushes and then took them to Mr. Russell. Prisoner also hawked about a plated dog collar and took it to Mr. Hatton. of the " Seven Stars" and also to a Mr. Whitby, and Left it with the latter on approval. On the same night he took a pocket knife and Fold that for 2d. He also sold a pair of stepladders to a Mr. George Parr for ls.3d. Mr. Davies did not wish to press tne case very strongly because everyone in Warrington was sorry for the friends and relatives of Bolton, wits also felt his position acutely. They left the matter entirely in the hands of the Bench. There was, he said, no excuse for the felony because everything must have been premeditated.—Mr. Davids mentioned that the prisoner had stolen Mr. Lowe's Beady Reckoaer, with his name in, off the desk. That was a most senseless thing to de. He must have known what he was doing in the othericasesbecause he had disposed of the articles at a time when he had not actually stolen them. Mr. Browne said he would take advantage of two or three words which Mr. Davies had used when he said the acts were senseless and insane. He,himself, said the same thing and hid been instructed to say that prisoner's friends could not understand why he should do it. His wife had told Mr. Browne that for some time prisoner had not been able to get a proper night's rest. During the sale he had taken drink and his relatives could only account for the thefts by believing that the drink,coupled with his weak state, most be the sole cause of his taking some of the things. Mr. Bolton did buy and sell, and during the sale he bought a quantity of things from people coinieg out of the shop. Some, however he had stolen and Mr. Browne asked their worships to take into consideration that Bolton was a Warrington man and 62 years of age and had been taken ill with rheumatism in the leg while at Strangeways gaol since the remand,and could hardly walk. Ho asked on behalf of the prisoner's wife and others for as much leniency as possible and re- marked that prisoner had never been committed for any other offence. . . .

The Chairman said the prosecution had put the matter in a mild form, and having regard to the prisoner's friends and the state of his health they were dealing more leniently with the case than they ought in sending him to goal for one month's hard labour. niS ill-health would be taken into consideration when the bard labour was enforced.

THE AMERICA CUP.

The cup which the Valkyrie has gone over to New York to try and bring back to this side of the Atlantic, says the " Sporting and Dramatic," is not, as many sup- pose, a Queen's Cup. The yacht America never won a Queen's Cup. The piece of plate which is called the America Cup was merely one of the prizes offered for competition at the Royal Yacht Squadron Regatta of 1851. It was won by the America by a mere accident, and under conditions which could not possibly prevail at the present day. Commodore Stevens, a prominent member of the New York Yacht Club, and his friends for she was a syndicate boat—built the America on pur- wee to race for the Queen's Cup at the Royal Yacht Squadron Regatta in 1851. But when she arrived in the Solent lie found that the Queen's Cup was only open to vessels belonging to the Squadron, and that the race was subject to time allowance. The committee in order to induce the American yacht to enter, threw the race ' "open to all the world," but declined to waive the time allowance. Consequently Commodore Stevens refused to en, er his yacht, and the race was sailed without the America, the Q ieen's Cup being won by the cutter Bacchante. The Squadron were as anxious as Commo- dore Stevens for a match, hut did not want to waive the time-allowance rule. Accordingly, they permitted the owner of the America to post a notice in the club house offering to sail any British yacht for a stake of 1.000 to 10,000 guineas, without time-allowance. Wnether it was because the prize was too great a sum to risk, or whether other objections stood in the way, it is certain that the challenge remained unac- cepted, and there appeared every chance of the Ameri- can yacht going back without a race. At this crisis the owner of the Titania, a 100-ton cutter, curiously enough belonging to a gentleman bearing the same name as the owner of the America, came to the rescue and offered a 1 ' sail fel. a prize of £lOO. The challenge was accepted and the day, fixed. The !loyal Yacht Squadrsn, however, I did not like the idea of a private match, and feared tbat it would he said they did not give the America a chance. I Consequently they decided that the Squadron race round ; the Isle of Wight should be without time allowance. I Whereupon the race with the Titania was abandoned i and the America entered for the snatch, and won the 1 prize which i‘, now known as the "America Cup."

THE SAVAGES OF FORMOSA.

Formosa, the island that China has ceded to Japan, deserves its name, says a writer in the " Journal dee Voyages," since it is of marvellous beauty. The Portu- guese called it Hermosa, and the English-speaking world has substituted for that name the Latin equivalent. Reclus, the French geographer, has described the physi- cal features of the island with something like enthus• iasm. The Chinese seized points on the coast in the fifteenth century. but never really conquered the and there was great danger of war between China and Japan twenty years ago, because the latter landed troops on the island to punish the outrages perpetrated by the savages of the interior. The story of many con- quests is written in successive zones of inhabitants in the island, peoples that became the victims of one con- quering tribe after another, and wore driven towards the interior because the last conqueror never held posses- sion of any part but the coast regions. The most savage people of Formosa are the moun- taineers called Igorrotes, or by the Chinsee, Song-Fan (savage-men). They are head-hunters, like their sup- posed relatives in Luzon, and their arms are spears, bows and arrows, and great kaives. The people of the plains are called Pepo-lioans. They resemble the Igor- rotes, but the latter are usually smaller, though some of the savage mountain tribes are noted for stature and agility. Their arms are very long, and their feet enorm- ous. They step upon the ground only with the front half of the foot, and seem to seize it in running. There is much variety of physiognomy among them, and their great rolling eyes inspire terror. They love to adorn themselves with copper bracelets, collars and belts of course glassware discs. The Igorrotes are famous for cruelty and ferocity, and they resort to ingenious stratagems for the discomfiture of their enemies. They have for toe coast dwellers a traditional hatred, like that of the highlander for the lowlander, and their descents are like those of the ‘VeNti and Scotch mountaineers of early days upon the more civilised people of the lowlands. The savages keep watch from their mountain-tops for the approach of toe impru- dent coast dwellers in search of wood or coal. When the lowlanders are seen at work, the savages glide down behind them by paths unknown to the strangers and fall Moon them with lance and knife. The heads of the victims are cut off and their bodies are left lying at the scene of the butchery. The heads are treated so that they may be kept as trophies, and when one of these people has taken a certain number of heads he obtains, by way of honour, the right to sell pipes. The pipes are bits of Wood representing the human head. They are decorated with bits of copper. The lgorrotes do not possess a written alphabet, but besides expressing something by tattooing, they keep the calen- dar by means of knotted strings. They thus keep count of the seasons and the years. But in spite of their ignorance of matters civilised, they have a deep cunning that aids their ferocity, and this they use in their frequent wars upon their more civilised brethren of the coast and upon the Cainese strangers. Tile island waters are specially rich in fish, and this has led to a e formation ef Chinese fishing villages upon the coast. These villages are apart from the settlements of the Pepo-hoans,thoush the latter and the Chinese are ordin- arily at peace. The Igorrotes some years ago formed a plan of attack upon some of the people of the coast. It was decided at a cmucil or the savages that they divide themselves into two bands, one to attack the Pepo-heans, who seemed to be too much in league with the invading Chinese, the other to destroy a Chinese fishing The descent was made in the middle of the night,tbe Pepo-hoans were slain or taken captive, all the crops were destroyed, and the savages retreated with their prisoners in order that none might be left at the scene ' of the massacre to tell the story. Meanwhile, a Chinese fishing village, five leagues away, was attacked and burned. So sudden WM the descent that the fishermen living in boats had not time to cut their moorings and escape. Only those absent fishing remained alive, and they did not dare to return to the burning village. Before leaving the repo-Ewan village the savages had dropped in various places articles that could be identi- fied as of Chinese origin. At the Chinese villiagc they likewise left relics of the Pepo-hoans, such as a gun of ancient pattern, rice measures, a hat made of bamboo leaves, and other such articles. Then after a cannibal- istic feast the savages returned to the mountains. The object of this stratagem was to spread the belief among each group of enemies that the other and not the moun- taineers had perpetrated the butchery. So well did the device work that for six months the Pepo-boans and the Chinese colonists were in a state of hostility.

An express train from London Bridge to Eastbourne on Saturday evening met with an accident which, but for the presence of mind of a guard, might have had latat results. As the train was approaching East Croydon station at xpress speed the slip guard's van,in the centre of a train ot nineteen carriages, fouled the pointt, and the Worthing section of the train, consisting of nine coaches, was thrown off the rails. The guard immedi- ately slipped off the front part of the train, and thus averted worse disaster. The passengers were only slightly injured. The permanent way and rolling stock were badly damaged.

Lettero to the eilitor. _

THE PECULIAR PEOPLE. Sir,—lona, in last issue, broaches a sullect of very ignorant William and great importance. The fact of poor William sable being prosecuted for faithfully and imph- citely following the teachings of the New Testament guy rise to reflections adverse to the idea of our nati inZ sanity and consistency. According to Blackstone, Christianity is part the Common of e Common Law of England ; and yet, for prac o the precepts of this part of the Common Law of England, this Common Law inflicts pains and penal'..les upon those who too strictly follow its injunctions. Now what con- clusion can we come to from the cor sideratien of such an insensate jumble of costradictior.as ? To my mind the only conclusion possible is that, it our sanity is to be granted, our honesty is imphe sted. In other Words, if we are sane we must. be a p of arrant humbugs to countenance these moons Ate„ies. Whoso arrayeth himself in his best" go .da meetings " store clothes and sits in chapel or Churc,,,.... and listens complacently while the Rev. Amminada.'d Tackletext expounds and expa- tiates upon the test "James v. 14-15.," and then comes forth into the world and proclaims himself as a law abiding citiven, then and there becomes tho hypocritical humbug vino pretends to serve two masters. Hence I stay that no sincere and conscientious Christian can con- instently occupy a seat on the magisterial bench, owing to his liability to be called upon to violate either his principles or his duty. From these considerations naturally arises the ques- tion, "How long are the ignorant imaginings and im- practical dogmas of the firs", and second centuries to dash with and nullify legislative efforts for the national welfare? It has been said that the American Congress is engaged one part of the year in passing laws to coun- teract the pernicious teaching of the pulpit during the other part of the year. I leave my readers to decide which—the pulpit or the Legislature—most requires the hand of the reformer. Closely connected with this subject is the much vexed Sunday otoservance question. Here again English Law imposes penalties for the non-observance of the First Day as a day of rest. Now there is not the slightest reference in any of the scriptures, new or old, oral or written, to the first day as being exceptionally holy or set apart for devotional observance, while there is of the seventh—our Saturday—which, and which alone, is the Sabbath of the Scriptures. Bence it comes about that Warrington folks cannot employ a barber to shave their chins on Sunday because a Pagan Emperor and a worshipper of the sun altered the day of rest from Saturday to Sunday —his day for worshipping the sun. Here is his edict by which the change was effected. "The Emperor Con- stantine Aug. to Helpidius. On the venerable day of the sun let the magistrates and people residing in cities rest, and all workshops be closed. In the country, how- ever, persons engaged in the work of cultivation may freely and lawfully continue their pursuits ; because it often happens that another day is not so suitable for grain sowing or vine planting ; lest by neglecting the popular moment for such operations the bounty of heaven should be lost. Given, the seventh day of March." (A.D. 321). No Sabbatarianism here; but sound Pagan common sense which throws an instruc- tive light on the Sunday observance fanaticism.— Yours, &c.,

JOHN PEERS.

SHIP CANAL

SERIOUS COLLISION.

On Wednesday morning a serious collision occurred on the Ship Canal at Acton Grange, near Runcorn. The passenger steamer Irene, coming down the canal, at- tempted to pass between the steamship Lobe, from Hamburg, and the steanitug Walton, towing two flats. The Irene struck the Lance on the port bow, then sheered off into the flats Trent and Mary Ellen, jamm- ing the latter against the railway bridge. All the craft were much damaged. The Mary Ellen was rendered practically worthless.

===== 0002601_18950907_art0102.txt | included=true | included =====
, RACING BETWEEN PASSENGER STEAMERS.

What appeared to be a race between two passenger steamers was witnessed on the Ship Canal at Runcorn on Sunday. The steamers had a full complement of pas- sengers, and for a greater portion of the distance between Weston and Runorn Bridge they went almost abreast at full speed,the first one gaining a few yards and then the other, amid the shouts of those on board. To witness the contest, the passengers on the larger vessel crowded tc one side with the result that it leaned greatly over, and if it had canted to the Mersey side, to which it was very near, the probabilities are that a calamity too fear- ful to contemplate would have happened. To add to the danger run, there were vessels and flats moored in the length of the canal in question, but fortunately no vessels coming in the opposite direction. LAST WEEK'S TRAFFIC.

During the seven days ending Friday the 30th August 180 seagoing vessels, with a total net registered tonnage of 43,967 tons, entered and cleared from the port of Man- chester, which includes all parts of the Ship Canal. The number of vessels using the canal during the previous week was 145, and the tonnage 38,995 tons. Appended is a table showing the proportion of coastwise and foreign traffic in the total : Inwards. Outwards. Ships. Ton. Ships. Tons. Coastwise 72 ... 13,815 Coastwise... 75 ... 10,721 Foreign --- Total... 91 22,406 Total... 89 21,561 Amongst the full cargoes which arrived were eight of ' China clay, four of wood pulp, three of timber, five of pig iron, three of stone, two of flints, one of butter, one of fruit, one of bog ore, and one of bone-ash whilst nine cargoes of coal, six of salt,twolof pitch,and one of purple, were exported. EFFECT OF THE CANAL ON THE TRADE OF HULL.

—- - - - A Hull corresponderf; writes :—The competition of the Manchester Ship Canal is being seriously felt at the east coast ports. At Hull the Camber of C-,mmerce has been approached by the merchants and shipownere, who are I keenly affected by the diversion of traffic to the west coast. It is asserted that Liverpool is now enjoying busi- ness that hitherto exclusively belonged to Hull, and that Manchester threatens to participate in, if not eventually to appropriate almost entirely, the trade formerly held by the port. One of the principals of a Bull firm regarded as being one of the largest private shipping firms in the country, during the course of an interview on Wednes- day, stated that it was undoubtedly a fact that Hull was being vitally affected by the canaLand he went so far as to say that he knew a firm of shipowners who would have given £50,000 to prevent the canal being constructed. He was interested in a railway company largely con- cerned in Hull, and he gave it as his opinion that that company would have given an enermous sum to have effected the same purpose. The pulp trade—one of the staple trades of Hull—has, he continned,been completely diverted, and this of itself was of the gravest interest to shipowners. He complained that there was unfair com- petition on the part of the canal authorities in making no dock charges or wharfage ; in addition to which ad- vantages tugs are supplied to steamers. "My own opinion is," he added, "that the position is very serious. but I don't see what can be done. The North Western Railway Company can only assist to this extent—taking off the dock charges at Hull, but we cannot hope for such a concession. Much against our will we are cern- pelled to send vessels to the canal, hut the outward traffic is only small. The question will, I believe, be brought before the North Western Railway clirectors,and in the meantime I don't think it wise that we should say much upon the matter."

===== 0002601_18950907_art0103.txt | included=true | included =====
THE CANAL COMPANY v. MESSRS.

FAIRCLOUGH.

DECISION OF THE ARBITRATOR.

The Manchester "Guardian" says :—Messrs. James Fairclorigh and Sons, of Warrington, who are the owners of a wharf and warehouse called Cooper's Wharf at Widnes, alleged that owing to the construction of the company's works a sand or mud bank had formed oppo- site the wharf, and that vessels could not navigate there- from or thereto as formerly, and that a change had also taken place in the riv,r Mersey, and claimed a large sum for compensation. The company denied that either the sand or mud bank or the change in the river was assign- able to their works. Mr. Flarrison Bayter was arpointed as arbitrator by the Board of Trade, and the question whether the formation of the mud or sand bank opposite Cooper's Wharf and the change in the river was to be assigned to the company's works was referred to him. The proceedings before the Arbitrator commenced on the 17th and terminated on the 25th of July last. Mr. Hay. ter has now made his award, and finds that the Canal Company have satisfied him that neither the existence of the mud or sand bank opposite Cooper's wharf nor the change in the river Mersey are caused by or are to be assigned to the company's works, either during or after the construction of the same, and he directs that Messrs. Faircloueh and Sons shall pay the company's costs of and incidental to the arbitration and the costs of the award, and that they shall bear their own costs of the same.

tBY AN OCCASIONAL CONTRIBUTOR).

don't know what has come over our District Council lately. Certainly things have not pro- I greased so happily as one could wish, but really the Council Chamber has recently provided as much amusement as a theatre when the boards are occu- pied by a comic opera. It was evidently an act of wisdom on the part of the majority to change the hours of meeting from six o'clock to four o'clock in the afternoon. In consequence of this the rate- payers have been debarred from having a little amusement at a cheap rate, and, in addition, some of the councillors have provided a means of pre- venting these same ratepayers from seeing their "goings on." If this sort of thing continues we shall be able to show a surplus in the next issue of the balance sheet, for certainly no money is being spent in improvements, but a large amount of valuable time is being wasted, and nothing at all in the shape of business is being transacted.

At the monthly meeeing last Monday with the exception of Messrs. Emmett and Stone, there was a full attendance of the Council, and consequently there was no fear of another hitch over the minutes, which were passed by the usual majority. It is a splendid majority, whose aims are directed solely to one end, and it is amusing to witness the peculiarity of the representatives of one particular ward expressing divergent views on the same topic.

When the Gas and Water minutes were brought up for confirmation there was a breezy interchange over the correspondence with Mr. Webb, with re- spect to the water supply. Mr. MacMahon wanted some more light on the subject,and wished to ascer- tain by whose authority the correspondence had taken place. Mr. Smith, who is the chairman of the committee, said it had been dons on the autho- rity of the sub-committee. Mr. MacMahon said he was a member of that committee, and hs knew nothing of the correspondence, and he felt ag- grieved that he had not been apprised of it, and he denounced what be called " these hole and corner meetings." 1 -- I don't know exactly what is intended to be done over this water question, but I am afraid if we are not very careful, the Newton end of the township is going to land us in a large expenditure. It is an old question and a sore point with the Newton people, and their idea is to discontinue getting water from the Railway Co., and obtain it by other means, possibily by sinking wells and erecting pumping plant. I don't know what the Newton people have to grumble at. Their supply is good, and what benefit they will derive from an altera- tion of the present arrangement, lam at a loss to comprehend. However, the matter is still in embryo, and we may hear something more about it later on.

On the School Attendance Committee minutes, Mr. MacMahon expressed himself strongly with re- gard to the report of the School Attendance Officer not being printed on the minutes, as was formerly the case, and plainly told the chairman that he could put his remarks in the resolution which be had moved, If he liked.

The Chairman added that he had received a letter from Father Lennon, dated August 22nd., which touched upon the same matter they had just dis- cussed, but he did not give the Council any idea ol its contents. Mr. Smith, took the strictly proper course of moving that the letter be referred to the Committee, but Mr. Gilmour objected and wanted the letter read there and then. Mr. MacMahon was of the same opinion, and Mr. Beatty rose in a very excited manner, and said if the letter was not read for the information of the public, he would with- draw from the committee, and he did so, taking himself off from the Council Chamber altogether. This little episode rather upset matters, and after deciding to held a special meeting to privately con- sider the matter on Wednesday night, the Council rose, after doing really nothing but squabble.

FOUNDATION STONE LAYING AT IIAYDOCK.

SPEECH BY LORD NEwroN.

Haydock was to some extent en fete on Saturday afternoon, at least that part of it which lies round about St. James' Church, the occasion being the laying of a foundation stone of an extension to the infant scho,l• room connected with St. James's Natural School, by Lord Newton. In addition to this it is proposed to pro- vide a large cloak-room, and to erect a wall dividing the school yard from the church. At nresent the accommo- dation of the Infant School is 150, and when the addi- tions completed, it is expected this will be increased to 220. The estimated cost of the alterations is £1.60, and the builders are Messrs. Mercer of Haydock, and the architect, Mr. T. Robinson, of Messrs. R. Evans and Company. In addition to this there was a b.,zaar in the larae schoolroom in support of the Church debt, at which Mrs.']. D. Grimke-Drayton performed the hon- ours, and particulars of which are given elsewhere. The whole scheme is set forth as follows :—" The debt on the debt on the building of the new church and the wall round the graveyard £7OO. (2) Considerable repairs to the Parish Church, an inspection of which fabrio will disclose the urgent necessity for immediate attention, for the wind blows through the apperture and the rain comes pouring in to the considerable damage of the organ and other things in the church. (3) Balaton Black- brook New Infant School, £9OO. (4) Curate's house for Blackbrook. (5) Restoration or building St. Alban's Mission Chapel, Blackbrook, which is in a ruinous con- dition. oID Building extrusion to infant school near Parish Church, £160." A procession was formed at the Flaydock reading-room and proceeded to the site headed by the Haydock brass band, and immediately followed by the clergy and cht,ir in surplices, Lord Newton and other gentlemen and ladies and scholars of the parish. Tne choir took up their place on a temporary platform, and surrounding the stone were, Revs. C. E. Bowden (vicar of Haydock), Rev. W. J. Melville (rector of Ashton), Rev. H. Siddall (vicar of Ashton). Rev. H. Monk (St. Peter's, Newton), Rev. T. R. Matthe ,V 1 (vicar of Earlestown). Rev. F. G. Wills (Unholland), Rev. A. Watkins, Lady Gerrartl,Elfat. Mrs. T. W. Leg'', Mrs. Gillespie. Dr. and Mrs. Street,Dr. and Mrs. Hayward, Mrs. Grimke-Drayton, Captain and Mrs. Royse, Mr. W. S. Barrett, J.P., Mr. R. and Mrs. Bradshaw, Mr. T. Robinson, Mr. C. B. F. Bnrron. Mr. and Mrs. John Jackson. Mr. and Mrs. James Taylor and Mr. A. Evans and Mr. E. Jackson (wardens).—The stone bore the following inscription:—" This stone was laid by the Right Hon. Lord Newton, on Saturday, August 31st, 1895. Bear ye one another's burdens and so fulfil the law of Christ." When all had assembled, Mr. A. Evans (warden), said he had taken the liberty of asking Lord Newton to open the proceedings that day, because in the first place, he (Mr. Evans) happened to be perhaps the only one present of the members of the committee for building the origi- nal parish church in Haydock, but there was another reason which perhaps many of tha".^. could not lay claim to, and that was he was a bred and born Haydock man. For those two reasons, he had obtained permission from the Vicar to address to Lord Newton in their hehalf,their sincere welcome for his presence that day, and to express their thanks for his kindness. The purpose for which they were met, although perhaps the sZ9 of the building was no; a consideration, was more far reaching,hecause they were laying the foundation of the future. (Hear, hear.) He would ask his lordahip'a ac- ceptance of a silver trowel with which to lay the stone. It could not be used as a butter knife, but he would ask him to use it as a paper cutter, as a remembrance of Haydock. He (Mr. Evans) was thankful to announce that between £BO and £9O had been promised towards the £l6O required, but Lord Newton had kindly added £25 to that amount, and £5O towards the church debt. (Applause.) They all knew that the Nonconformists at the other end of Haydock had received kindnesses from his lordship, and had erected a fine pile of buildings, which had put the church people to shame. Be then handed the trowel to Lord Newton. .

His lordship said he thought at the present time edu- cation in this country was going through a very great phase. They had to consider that during the last year or two great difficulties had been thrown upon voluntary schools throughout the country. Managers ILO gone through a good deal of suffering at the hands of those who ought never to have put it upon their shoulders. During the last two years, under compulsory powers, enormous sums had been raised in this country, but the pleasing part of it was perhaps the little effect it had had upon that policy which so many who had a strong religious feeling had supported, namely, religious teach- ing in voluntary Schools. From the attacks which had been made It appeared as if the supporters of undenomi- national schools wished to upset denominational schools in order to carry out what he might say was a fad on many sides. Greater support ought to be given to the denominational schools than they had received of late years, and at the same time the consciences of Dissen- ters who disapproved and wished their children to he taught their own religion should be safeguarded. This education might, he thought, be carried on fairly to all parties. Lord Newton then laid the st.,ne, and a short service was gone through by the Rey. C. E. Bowden, the choir singing the hymns " We are but little children," and "There's a Friend for little children."

The above choir held their mutual picnic on Saturday. The party numbering about 40, with several friends, lett Earlestown by the 5.55 train on Saturday morning for Blackpool, in charge of Messrs. WEDDING AT NEWTON.

At St. Peter's Church, Newton, on Wednesday morning there was a large gathering to witness the marriage of Miss Louise Houghton, eldest daughter of Mr. Henry Houghton. assistant overseer, Park- road, Newton, with Mr. Arthur Wilson Anderton of Hazel Mount, Earlestown. The ceremony was per- formed by the Rev. IC Monk ( vicar), and the bridesmaids were Miss Annie M. Houghton, and Miss Egerton (cousin of the bride), while the best man was Mr. T. Egerton. The bride was given away by her father, and after the breakfast, which was held at Mr. Houghton's, the happy couple left for London for the honeymoon. The presents were numerous and costly.

RUNCORN SCHOOL BOARD.

A meeting of this Board was held in the Board room at the Town Hall, Runcorn, on Tuesday evening. The chair was occupied by Mr. J. Pritchard, and there were also present : Rev. Dr. Grastaldi, Rev. Canon Wood, Mr. E. Imison, J.P., Dr. McDougall, and Mr. J. Moss (clerk).

===== 0002601_18950907_art0115.txt | included=true | included =====
THE CLERK'S SALARY.

The Chairman in moving the confirmation of the corn- mittee'a minutes granting an increase of £25 per annum to the Clerk, stated that the matter was well considered in committee. He believed the Clerk had served the Beard for the past six years at the rate of salary which he was now receiving, and had not the duties increased there would have been no call for an increase of salary. The increased duties were particularly with regard to the taking over, and having the control of 600 more children who had been attending the Wesleyan schools. This responsibility would greatly increase the Clerk's duties, and after considering the matter very carefully, they thought he was entitled to an increase of £25. The Rev. Canon Wood, in seconding the proposition, I said he believed that the amount proposed to be given was not realty proportionate to that which had been paid for the work which had been done previously. To explain his meaning he might say that the work of the School Board was two kinds. There was the attend- ance of children throughout the whole town to be looked after, and it was also their duty to manage certain schools. It appeared that some years ago the Board had only one branch of the work, that of seeing to the chil- dren, and in those days the Clerk was paid £6O. Since that time the amount which bad been added to the Clerk's salary in consideration of the extra work con- nected with the Board Schools, had been £4O. If the Board had gone on in the same proportion, considering that the same number of children had been added to the case of t•ie Biard,thev would have advanced the Clerk's salary from £lOO to £l4O. One could not help noticing that they hen to pay for work which was formerly done without pay. The Wesleyan Committee who had man- aged the schools previously did the work for nothing but as soon as it fell on the shoulders of the Board and the Clerk, and it was altogether on a different footing. It was not now voluntary work and no one wished to underpay their servants.lt was a great misfortune when the voluntary schools were thrown on the rates. It could not take place without an increase of rates. It all the voluntary schools were to be thrown on the rates no doubt their clerk would find himself in possession of a larger salary, which he did not think would be a mat- ter of satisfactipn to the ratepayers. Dr. Gastaldi thought the time was not opportune to raise any salaries. Runcorn was decidedly in a miser- able condition, and during the present week 60 persons were summoned for non-payment of rates. He had op- posed the proposal in committee,and he moved now that the question be def ,rred to six months. Mr. Imison supported the proposition and he thought it was a reasonable one.

The Chairman pointed out that shortly, the Board would be involved in the erection of new schools which would be another addition to the work of the Clerk. It had been their desire to do that which was equally just between the Clerk and the ratepayers, and he thought the amount proposed was not excessive. As the amendment was not seconded it fell to the ground, and the proposition was carried, the only dis- sentient being Dr. Gastaldi.

THE ATTENDANCE OF SCHOLARS. The report of the school attendance officers showed that the number on the rolls was 3.474 as compared with 3488, in the corresponding period last year, being an increase of six. The average attendance was 2,782 as compared with 2,803 last year. being a decrease of 21. The percentage of attendance was 80 as against 81, twelve months ago. THIS EXAMINATIONS, FEES, &C _

A communication was read from the Education De- partment in whica they stated that they had sanctioned on the special recommendation of Her Majesty's Inspec- tor, the omission of the annual inspection of the Green- way-road Board School, due in July 1896. Another letter was also read from the Education Department ap- proving of the following scale of fees: infants free ; girls' school, 1,2, and 3 standards, penny per week ; 4th to 7th standards,2l per week ; boys, standards 1,2. and 3 penny per week; 4, and 5,2 d. per week ; 6, and 7,3 d. per week. The approval is given subject to the condi- tion that the total amount of fees received in any school shall not exceed the maximuna amount allowable under that section, the amount of such excess to be taken in reduction of tha fee grant for such school year. This concluded the business.

LOCAL LIBERALS AT DRUID'S CROSS.

CELEBRATING THE VICTORY.

In order to celebrate the triumphant return of Sir John T. Brunner, Bart., to the House of Commons, between 200 and 300 Liberals in the Northwich division had an excursion to Druid'sCross,Wavertree,the seat of Sir John, on Monday. The intention had been to spend the entire day at Druid's Cross, but on it transpiring that Sir John would be engaged until afternoon.Mr.J. T. Yearsley and Mr. F. W. Brock had an interview with Mr. Shaw, the district superintendent of the L. and N.-W.Railway Co., who issued instructions that the excursionists could visit Liverpool and proceed to Msssley Hill for Wavertree by the 2.40 and 2.50 trains. These arrangements were car- ried out, and the party on arrival at Druid's Craig was received with great cordiality by Sir John and Lady Brunner and the memberS of their family, Most elabo- rate arrangements had been made to promote the enjoy- ment of aI. In an extensive marquee refreshments of the choicest kinds were laid out. All were welcome,and the genial homeliness of Sir John and Lady Brunner did much to set at ease all with whom they came into con- t mt. The member for North wich escorted party after plity through the hall, pointing out and explaining at the request of the sight-seers dainty bric.a brac, rare pic- tures, quaint curios, and not the least interesting the many handsome testimonials of the esteem and regard in which their owner is held. The gardens and conser- vatories with their wealth of alamanda, begonia' station probisa, palms, tree ferns, Ste. (all beautifully kept by Mr. James Robinson and his assistants) came in for their share of laudation. Mr. W. P. Bailey's string band was in attenda.nce,and discoursed sweet music. Prior to the excursio, ists leaving in order to catch the 1.7 p.m.train from M .ssley Sir J. T. Brunner, M.P., delivered a short speech. He said : It is a great pleasure to us hero, being at such a distance from our old .home, to see so many pleasant and familiar faces around Ili. It was a great grief to us to have our old home in Cheshire, where we had brought up our children so happily together, to live here ; hut I can say it here in my wife's presence that it was not for our own sake that we came avray,but it was for the sake of the children themselves, because we found nat they would be better off near a large town than they were at Winnington ; and then as all of you know, the works have rather crowded close to the old home, so that there Was not as much room for us there as we liked to have. I have to thank you all, both tke ladies and the gentle- men, for I know that a very large number of ladies did splendid work, for puling me again into that position of which I am proud and of which I always have been proud. (Cheers ; a voice: "1,638" ; another voice : " 2,000 next time " ; a third : " The only bright spot in Cheshire.") Ladies end gentlemen, I do not know whether we can look forward with any certainty to a larger majority next time,for the reason that I am rather inclined to think that the thorough beating that you gave to our opponents on this last eceasion will have so damped their ardour that the probability is that We Shall not have a fight for many a day to come. (A Voce : " A walk-over.") I am told,and it is perfectly trile,that that means a majority not of 2.000, bat of 12,000 (Cheers.) Ladies and eentlemen, I hope that the next time we may meet On these grounds is net very far off —(hear, hear)—and I shall he glad indeed if you will all encourage your friends and neighbours to join you when you come, and that we may see very many more of our friends than we have to-day. (Cheers.) Two whom I have met have been good enough to bring two others each, and if yen will only follow the same lexample and each bring two friends—(a Voice ; " Tories ?")—it went matter to us whether they are Tories—(laughter)—because I am happy to say, that with very few exceptions in the whole of the constitu- ency, I find that the Tories are my friends, almost, I wont say as well as, but almost am well as the Liberals. (Cheers, and a Voice: " All but voting." Laughter.) Mr. Philip Speakman, of Runcorn, said : Ladies and gentlemen,—We are all very happy to-day and I think it enhances the happiness that we have when Sir John and Lady Brunner were made a very happy couple. I assure von that the first day.we met in Northwich I felt sure of victory, but I was not so sure of the victory that we got ; hit that does not matter. It is a very signiff- cant fact that when our friends had tried against us we ' have each time incr 'wed Sir John's majority. I feel sure that as long as Sir John Brunner remains true to us, AS he has done for the last nine years, he need never be afraid of not having a seat in Parliament. (Cheers.) I think it is very nice, after the very arduous fight that we had, that WP Sh,"Alld meet here to rejoice with Sir John and Lady Brunner at their own home, and to feel at one with them, and to hope that continued happiness may he their lot as well as ours. (Cheers.) Ido not think we ought to separate n ow, after hearing Sir John Brunner, without wishing them hearty pleasure and Bod-Qpeed to the holiday so well deserved. (Loud

cheers.) ftinginz cheers were giv,m for Sir John and Lady Brunner and the members of their family, for which the hon. baronet returned thanks.

RUNCORN. BRAVE CONDUCT OF A RUNCORN YOUTH.

On Tuesday at Runcorn Police Court, Mr. J. E. Worsley, coroner, held an inquest on the body of William Riley, aged 17 years, son of Thomas Riley, pork butcher, Bridge-street, who was drowned in the Mersey at Run- corn on the previous day.—The father gave evidence of identification, and said he was not aware his son could swim.—Ja Ines Donald. 16 years of age, who resides in St. John-street, deposed that at noon he saw the deceased enter the Mersey at the Old Quay. Witness shouted, " Billy, can you swim ? " and he replied, "Oh, yes, I can swim." When deceased had got about 28 yards from the shore he threw up his hands. Witness at once pulled off his shoes and rushed into the water wita his clothing on. and he had swum to within ten yards of the deceased whea the latter sank and did not rise again. There was a strong ebb tide running, and also a heavy overflow from the Ship Canal weir,and witness had great difficulty in regaining the shore,as his clothes were very heavy.—The Coroner: Are you a good swimmer ? I am a fair swimmer,not to say a good one.—Peter Furneval and Walter Yates, youths,deposed to the recovery of the body.—The Coroner, in summing up, said Donald had evinced a very great amount of good feeling and bravery in acting as he did.—The jury coincided with the coroner's remarks, and the foreman (Mr.W.Wright) said the place was a very dangerous one. A verdict was returned to the effect that the deceased was drowned while bathing.—Subsequently the coroner and the foreman went to view the spot, with the inten- tion of drawing up a memorial to the Humane Society. The funeral of the deceased took place on Thursday afternoon in Runcorn Cemetery. There was a large at- tendance of sympathisers and friends. The Rev. Dr. Preston who was the officiating clergyman, in the course of a short address in Holy Trinity Church referred to the suddenness with which the deceased had been called away. In the procession to the Cemetery there walked in advance, the members of the Rose of Sharon Lodge of the Grand United Order of Oddfellows, amongst whom were Bros. S. Worrell, sick visitor, Edward Jones, trus- tee; and P.G.M. Joseph E. Jones, auditor. Mr. Edward Price and the members of his class at Holy Trinity school also walked, the deceased having been aesociated with it. The bearers were Messrs. J. Shaw, Richard Wiekstead, James Blundell, Frederick Lamb, Fred Platt, and J. Blundell, representing the moulding department at Messrs. Timmins foundry. The relatives and friends followed. A number of beautiful wreaths had been sent, contributed by mother and father, sister, Cissie, Mr. and Mrs. John Riley (aunt and uncle), Ernest and John Riley, Mr. and Mrs. Lowe, Mr. Charles Dutton, Mr. and Mrs. Yates and family. Mr. James Griffiths, fellow workmen at the foundry, Mr. Edward Prince, and the scholars of the Sunday school class. The funeral service of the Order of Oddfellows was read by Mr. Jones.

RUNCORN URBAN DISTRICT COUNCIL.

A meeting of this Council was held at the Town Hall, Runcorn, on Thursday morning. Mr. E. Imison, J.P., occupied the chair, and there were also present, Rev. Dr. Gastaldi, Messrs. S. Taylor, J.P.,C.C., J. W. Lightburn, H. J. Once, J. Jones, G. H. Kirkham, M. Sweetman, T. Dean, F. Wiswall, J. Wilson; Mr. F. A. Lake (law clerk). Mr. Marshall (secretary), Dr. McDougall (medical officer), and Mr. M. Barker (surveyor).

. , MEDICAL OFFICER'S REPORT. Dr. McDougall in his report for the month stated that the number of deaths registered amounted to 44. one of which was an inquest case. Of the remaining 43 cases, 3 were due to mesenteric disease, 3to paralysis, 13 to diarrl,cei, 17 to measles. 2 to convulsions, and one each to phthisis, inflammation of the living membiaue of the brain, debility, cystitis, and one to heart affection. The 44 deaths showed an average death rate of 27-7 per thousand of the estimated population for 12 months, and exclusive of the inquest case, of the 271. 19 were infants twelve months or under, and 15 were over 12 months and under 27 years. 56 births were registered during the month.—The report was adopted.

HIGHWAYS AND BUILDINGS COMMITTEE. This committee recommended that Mr. James Wilding be appointed assistant surveyor for a further term of two years at a salary after the rate of £l5O a year and that the term date from the Ist October next ; and that he devote his time principally to the superintendence of the making of private streets and to the work incidental thereto ; that the plan of Mr. John Hudson of four cottages proposed to be built in Dale-st., be approved.—The minutes were passed without com- ment, on the motion of Mr. Taylor, seconded by the Rev. Dr. Gastaldi.

THE LIBRARIAN'S EXPENSES TO CARDIFF.

It was recommended by the Library Cemmittee that 100 copies of the annual report be printed and that the Librarian attend the annual meeting of the Library Association of the United Kingdom to represent the Library Committee and that his expenses be allowed,the same not to exceed £s.—Mr. Grice moved the adoption of these minutes and Mr. Taylor seconded.—Me. Sweet- man moved an amendment that the last recolinmenda- tion be not passed. He said considering the state of the tlwn owing to the depression in trade, he thought they ought not to waste ZS on that matter.—Dr. Gastaldi seconded the amendment.—Mr. Liehtburn in supporting the confirmation of the minutes, said Mr. Speakman ob- jected to the money being wasted. He (Mr. Lightburn) did not think that the money would be wasted. By allowing their officer to attaid the conference they made him more efficient,and everything expended on the effi- ciency of their servants was money well spent.—Mr. Dan said he took a different view of the matter. He took it that their librari in was efficient, and he did not see the necessity of spending public money in sending him to such a conference.—Mr. Wiswall said if the spending of this money would tend to increase the rates he could understand this cry about bad times. The com- mittee could not spend more than the penny rate which was allowed. He considered that the £5 would be well spent. He could inform them that Sir Jahn Brunner had told them that if they wanted any books to help students attending the, Institute he would be very pleased to eet the books for them—(hear, hear)—and make a presnat of them. Surely the Council was not going to be so stingy in the face of such an offer.—Mr. Taylor said the granting of the money would not effect the rates at all, and further the Library Committee con- sisted of gentlemen who had taken very great interest in the s library work and he did not think for a msment that they would spend £5 if it were not for the advantage cf the library. On being put to the vote, the amendment was carried by five votes to four. For the amendment there voted Rev. Dr. Gastaldi, Messrs. Jones, Kirkham, Sweetsnan, and Ddan, against Messrs. Wiswall, Taylor, Lightburn and (=rice. Mr. Wilson and the Chairman did not vote.— The Chairman said it was quite Mrn for anyone to move a further amendment.—Mr. Grice moved that the Librarian attend the meeting and that his expenses be allowecl.—No seconder being found, the amendment was dropped.—Mr. Wiswall moved that the expenses allowed should not (moped £4 10s., and said it was not the time to be stingy and shabby. If they had no enterprise the trade of the town would be bad.—Mr. Lightburn seeonded the amend- ment, and said he took the higher ground of the utility of the conference.—Mr. Taylor supported the amendment.—Dr. Gastaldi referring to the good said to be derived from attending the conference, stated that the reports would be in the newspapers, and the Librarian could make himself acquainted with the pro- ceedings in that way. He knew his duties as well as any librarian in the kingdom.—Mr. Grice also spoke in favour of the amendment, but on being put to the vote it was Inst. The minutes with the exception of the one referring to the payment of the expenses of the librarian were then passed.

He thought it would 13,_‘ better in the interests of the Council if the notices were eiven in that way. This concluded the business.

The new steamer Georgic, of the White Star line, the largest freight carrying steamer in the world, has com- memorated her maiden voyage by the re cue of the crew of the disabled barque Toni, a German vessel, bound for England with deals. The crew, twelve in number, were taksn off by the Georgic ana landed safely in New York.

- A shocking occurrence took placa at Aston, Birming- ham, on Thursday. A man named Thomas Hancox was putting in some drain pipes at a new mission hall when the earth above him gave way and fell upon him. He was buried at a depth of fourteen feet, and although the earth was dug out in half an hour, he was dead when his body came into view.

===== 0002601_18950907_art0126.txt | included=true | included =====
RUNCORN.

THINGS HEARD AND SEEN AT

BY OUR RUNCORN CORRESPONDENT.

The long anticipated opening of the Runcorn Technical Institute has now become an accom- plished fact, and from henceforth, as was stated by Sir John Brunner, M.P., at the opening ceremony, it is consecrated to the use of the public of Run- corn for all time to come. This latest educational facility in the town is one that has been much needed and there is every probability of its being put to good use. It was pleasing to notice the presence at the opening ceremony of the large number of friends of education from various parts of the county, but on the other hand there were very fow formerly familiar faces in Runcorn to be seen—in fact the men who have amassed wealth in the town have departed, and seem to have shaken off the Runcorn dust from their feet, to return no more. Canon Wood hit the nail on the head when he said, when thanking Sir John Brunner for his assistance, that Runcorn people were not used to generosity en the part of the richer people ; if any- thing wanted doing they had to go to the poor.

A united effort will now be necessary to wipe off the adverse balance of .51,500 which is due on the building. It is a matter of course to say that if this is allowed to remain it will greatly cripple the efforts of the Technical Instruction Committee. The _whole of the grant of the Cheshire County Council and the additional penny rate,altogether amounting to about £513 will be necessary for the successful carrying on of the institution year by year, and therefore it behoves all who have at heart the pro- vision of better educational facilities of the people, to use a hackneyed phrase, to put their shoulders to the wheel, and place the institution on a more solid basis.

At the meeting of the Runcorn School Board on Tuesday, Canon Wood made some remarks which under the circumstances it was perhaps only quite natural to expect him to make. It was when speaking in support of the increase of the clerk's salary for the additional duties, con- sequent on the transfer of the Wesleyan day schools to the Board, and the work of managing which will now devolve upon the Clerk. They were now paying for work, "he generously in- formed us,' wbich had been done for nothing under the voluntary system, and in his opinion, "it was a great misfortune when the voluntary schools were thrown on the rates." We suppose he is speak- ing now from a ratepayers standpoint only,for later day experience points conclusively to the fact that Board schools are becoming more and more popular with the general public, and they show their pre- ference for them by sending their children there when they have the opportunity. Only last week this was brought before the notice of the members of the Runcorn Board by the letter (rum the Educa- tion D partment in which they stated that the girls' selools under the control of the Board were overcrowded. Yet, forsooth, in some of the de- nominational schools in the town, as was admitted, there was plenty of room !

The drowning fatality which occurred in the Mersey at Runcorn this week, again draws atten- tion to the necessity of suitable baths in the town. Boys will be boys, and if proper bathing accommo- dation is not provided they will utilise other places, whether dangerous or not. There is not much to be said in favour of a swim in the foul river Mersey— at Runcorn at any rate, or for that matter,the Ship Canal either,which is very little better. But youths are to be seen daily during the warm weather tak- ing a dip into them, and not a few have paid the penalty with their lives. There are no safeguards against drowning, and it is not always that people are near at hand to rescue in case of need. Mr. Walter Collier has again and again advocated the necessity of having baths, but has met with meagre support. The latest move in this direction has been the suggested baths underneath the proposed new Board schools. Whether the suggestion will be car- ried out, remains to be seen.

From the appearance of the agenda for the meet- ing of the Urban Council which was held yester- day, there was every promise of a quick despatch of the business, and very little to talk about. But the unexpected happened, and that brief recom- mendation of the Library Committee that the Librazian should attend the annual meeting of the Library Association, and that he be allowed ex- penses not exceeding .£s,did not escape the vigilant eye of some economical councillors, and it proved to be quite a fruitful source of debate. The re- sult ot the voting was also a surprise to some, for it was decided that no expenses be allowed. This seems rather hard upon the Librarian, and savours somewhat of a cheeseparing policy.

===== 0002601_18950907_art0127.txt | included=true | included =====
GROSS CRUELTY TO A. CHILD AT FRODSHAM. A HEARTLESS MOTHER.

At the Frodsham Police Court on Thursday, before Mars. C. Reynolds and T. Rigby, Emma Nickson, of no settled alsode, was charged with neglecting and abandoning her infant child, Wilfrid Nickson, aged five months on the night of September 2nd. Inspector McAteer N.S.P.C.C. stated that on the 2nd of this month, the defendant, and her illegitimate child were brought to the police office. P.C. Price asked the mother to undress it, and then it was seen that the child was thoroughly emaciated. The bones on the back, arms, and legs, were devoid of flesh, and the child was almost a skeleton. The officer asked the mother how she accounted for the condition of the child and she told him she did not know nor care anything about it. She also refused to go into the workhouse, saying that she would rather see the thing die in the hedges than go there. As she left the police officer she said that she would see the child dead that night. Late the same night, the child was found on a doerstep in Maine-st.. Frodsham, and the case was reported to the poiice, station.—P.C. Clays immediately went in pursuit of the defendant and arrested her four miles away,and brought her back to Frodsham. The child only weighed eight pounds, but ought to weigh nibs Boz. The defendant had been continually sleeping out at night on doorsteps and underneath hedges for the past three monthssand the child had been grossly and systematically neglected. On one occasion, the child was found lying on a doorstep crying bitterly. The Inspector asked for a substantial punishment to be inflicted upon the defendant as it was a most serious case, and the defendant must have been trying to starve the child to death. P.C. Clays said that when he arrested the defendant and charged her with the offence, she replied "Yes, it can go to h—." About three weeks previously, the defendant was seat to gaol for 14 days for being found sleeping with the child upon a doorstep. Be had re- peatedly cautioned her. _ . . . . . _ _

john Baird also deposed to seeing the child saturated with rain on one occasion. Elizabeth Wade gave corroborated evidence, and some very damaging evidence was given by P.C. Price. The Bench characterised the case as an extraordinary one, and sentenced the defendant to three months hard labour, and ordered the child to be handed over to the custody of the N.S P.C.C., until it was 14 years of age.

HALTON.

MEETING OF ODDFELLOWS.—A meeting of the Runcorn district was held at the Earl of Chester Lodge, Halton, on Thursday. The officers and delegates were present as follows :—G.M. J. Hartland, C. secretary, G. Burrows, P.P.G.M. ; treasurer, R. Wright, P.P.G.M.; trustee, W. Cringle, P.P.G.M. ; and auditors, P.G. W. E. Dudley and P.G. R. Jeacock. Delegates, Royal Oak Lodge, Widnes: P.P.G.M. W. Hartland, P. '.G.M. W. Fausey, P.G. F. Hartland,P.G. S. Rowe, P.G. J. Beech. William the Fourth, Runcorn P.G. J. Groome, Roland Jones, and J. Jones. Kent's Pride, Widnes: P.G. G. Moss, and G. Booth. Duke of Devonshire Lodge: P.P.G..M G. Rutter and P.G. Samuel Boyers. Ebenezer Lodge: J. Hilton. P.G. Earl of Chester Lodge: H. Dale. P.P.G.M. The purple degree was conferred upon S. Boyers, J. Jones, and R. Jeacock. The accounts were passed, and show the district to be in a thorough-going financial colidition and the membership had increased by 17. P.P.G. Masters R. Wright and G. Rutter gave a very interest- ing report of their visit to the Swansea A.M.C. at Whit- suntide. It was unanimously decided to pay the death claim on behalf of the late Captain Roberts, who was lost at sea in December last. According to the usual custom the nominations for the various offices were taken, namely :—G.M., Job Hartland, Royal Oak Lodge, Widnes; D.G.M., P.G.', R. Jones, H. Walton, W. E. Barrow, P.G. W. E. Dudley. This concluded the business of a public nature.

A daring attempt was made on Thursday afternoon to explode a bomb on the bank premises of Messrs. Roths- child in the Rue Lafitte, Paris. The perpetrator of the outrage, whose movements had excited the suspicion of a police officer and had caused him to be watched, was arrested on the spot aftes a severe struggle. He declared himself an Anarchist.

NO STOMACH MEDICINE, NO ELECTRIC- ITY. Sent in envolope on receipt of two stamps for postage A. J. LE6II. 92-93, Great Russell-street. London. W.O A BOOK FOR MARRIED WOMEN. The information contained in this book ought to be known by every married woman, aud it will not harm the unmarriep to read. The book is conveniently divided into 12 , chapters The first chapter treats of the changes at puberty, or when a girl becomes a woman. The second chapter treats of marriage from a doctor's standpoint ; points out thy best age for marriage, and who should have children and who not, and furnishes useful information that one can ordinarily get only from an intelligent doctor. The third chapter treats of the marriage of blood relations; and condemns such mar riages as a rule. Chapter ur treats of the signs of pregnancy. The fif th chapter tells w a woman should live during the pregnant state. Tue sixth chapter treats of mishaps and how to avoid them. seventh chapter treats of maternal impressions and shows that birthmarks are not due to longings in the part of the mother, but rather to her poor health. The eighth chapter teaches how to have easy confinements. Certain people believe that women should bring forth in pain and trouble, but the hygienic physician says that confinements can be made comparatively easy i certain rules are obeyed ; these rules are given. The nint chapter treats of the proper management of confinement until the baby is born. The tenth chapter tells how to treat the mother till she is up and about again. The eleventh chapter treats of sterility ; gives the main causes of it, how these may be overcome and children result The last chapter treats of the " change," a most important article for all women over forty. The book is full of useful information and no book is written which goes Bo thoroughly into matters relating to married women. Some may think too much is told ; such can scarcely be the case, for knowledge is power and the means of attaining happiness. T e book can be ha from Dr. T. R. ALLINSON, 25 Box, 4, Spanish-place. THE IRISH DISAGREEMENTS. MR. JUSTIN McCARTHY SPEAKS OUT. The "Freeman's Journal" published on Monday a strongly worded manifesto addressed by Mr. Justin McCarthy to the electors of South Kerry. The nomina- tion of Mr. William Murphy in opposition to Mr. T. G. Farrell brought, ha said, the whole people of Ireland face to face with a momentous issue. That issue was whether a party bound by its pledge to discipline and to unity was to perish or to be maintained. For three years there had been an incessant campaign against the unity of the Irish party. Every effort had been made to des- troy the national movement both in Ireland and America. "Every loyal supporter of party unity has been assailed," the manifesto continues, " by charges as foul as heinous as have appeared in the columns of the "Times when it was under the inspiration of Pigott. Finally, the campaign reached its climax when, in the middle of the general election, Mr. Healy uttered at Omagh the baseless falsehood that I and the Committ,e of the party had sold Nationalist seats for English gold. This is a state of things which car.not go on. It would be impossible for any man or body of men successfully to carry on a movement if such disloyalty were tolerated, and unless the Irish people declare in unmistakable lan- guage their resolve te put down this new faction a united party cannot be mentioned."
